              "color": base_color,
              "roughness": 0.45,
              "metalness": 0.85,
              "roughnessMap": rough["texture"],
          })
          # Apply cavity-dirt as a multiplied AO slot if the
          # material supports it (MeshStandardMaterial doesn't;
          # MeshPhysicalMaterial does via aoMap):
          return mat

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 246 — 6 RIGGING TECHNIQUES (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 148)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  1. PIVOT OFFSETS = MESH LENGTHS
     The Python equivalent of PART 148.1: pivot positions are derived
     from the parent mesh's bounding box, not magic numbers. Use
     `mesh.geometry.boundingBox` (computed via `.computeBoundingBox()`)
     and place child pivots at `±(parentMax * factor)` on the
     appropriate axis.

         # Compute parent bbox
         parent_mesh.geometry.computeBoundingBox()
         bb = parent_mesh.geometry.boundingBox
         torso_width = bb.max.x  # half-extent is bb.max.x - bb.min.x
         arm_pivot.position.set(-(torso_width * 0.9), 0.1, 0)  # PART 251
         # No more `arm_pivot.position.set(-0.28, 0.1, 0)` magic numbers.

  2. HIERARCHY = ANATOMY
     Build the Group hierarchy to mirror anatomy. `body_pivot`
     children are `head_pivot`, `arm_L_pivot`, `arm_R_pivot`,
     `leg_L_pivot`, `leg_R_pivot`. The sculptRuntime.nodes map
     reflects this hierarchy (so the inspector's tree view is
     useful).

  3. NAMING CONVENTION
     Python factory names use snake_case (Python convention):
       `body_pivot`, `arm_L_pivot`, `wheel_front_L`, `wheel_front_R`.
     The renderer's inspector accepts both `arm_L_pivot` and
     `arm_l_pivot` (case-insensitive), but the lbl helper's
     `lbl.create_anchor("arm_L_pivot")` form is canonical.

  4. RIGID vs SMOOTH BINDING
     For low-poly Python factories, the model is usually a single
     Group of static meshes — no skinning needed. If the model
     has skinning (a character with bones), set up SkinnedMesh:
         sk_mesh = THREE.SkinnedMesh.new(geom, mat)
         sk_mesh.add(bones[0]); sk_mesh.add(bones[1]); ...
         sk_mesh.bind(skeleton, bones)
     Most chunky-mascot Python factories skip this.

  5. AUTO-RIG VALIDATION
     Run the validator's PART 207 sculptRuntime contract: every
     `detailInventory` entry must have at least one matching Mesh
     in the userData tree. The validator's PART 48 audit surfaces
     missing parts as warnings.

  6. SOCKET SYSTEM
     For modular attach points, use `lbl.create_anchor(id, position)`:
         lbl.create_anchor("head_socket", position=(0, 0.6, 0))
     Anchors appear in the inspector's tree view as floating points
     that other parts can be parented to.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 247 — 6 ANIMATION TECHNIQUES (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 149)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  1. REST POSE FIRST
     Build the model in its rest pose (T-pose / A-pose for characters,
     level pose for vehicles). The animation then DEVIATES from rest,
     not approaches it. This makes the `tick()` math simple.

  2. PHASE-OFFSET
     For walk cycles, phase-offset left/right limbs by 0.5:
         def tick(dt, elapsed):
             phase = elapsed * 2.0  # 2 rad/s
             arm_L.rotation.x = math.sin(phase) * 0.4
             arm_R.rotation.x = math.sin(phase + math.pi) * 0.4
     The Pi phase shift makes the right arm swing opposite to the
     left, which is the natural quadruped / biped pattern.

  3. NODENAME.PROPERTY TRACK NAMES
     For Three.js AnimationMixer tracks, name the target as
     `nodename.property`. The renderer's inspector reads these.
     Example:
         track_name = "arm_L_pivot.rotation"
     NOT `"arm L rotation"` (no spaces, use underscores).

  4. LOOP FRAME EQUALITY
     The first and last frames of a loop animation must be EQUAL
     (not just close — exactly equal). Otherwise you get a visible
     "pop" at the loop point. Test by reading the last frame's
     value and setting it on the first frame explicitly.

  5. HEADLESS PLAYBACK TEST
     The renderer's `lblAnim.mixer.update(dt)` runs in the page,
     but you can test the math headlessly:
         for i in range(60):
             t = i / 60.0
             phase = t * 2.0
             assert abs(math.sin(phase + math.pi) - (-math.sin(phase))) < 1e-9
     If the math is wrong here, it'll be wrong in the browser.

  6. SECONDARY MOTION
     Body parts that should follow the main motion (a tail following
     a body, an antenna following a head) get a damped second-order
     spring:
         def tick(dt, elapsed):
             target_x = math.sin(elapsed) * 0.5
             spring.target_x = target_x
             spring.step(dt)
             tail.rotation.x = spring.value
     Implement spring as a Verlet-integrated 2nd-order system
     (target + velocity, with damping).

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 248 — 6 VALIDATION & QA TECHNIQUES (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 150)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  1. SILHOUETTE FROM 4 ANGLES
     The renderer captures the silhouette from 4 angles (front, side,
     top, 3/4) automatically. You don't need to do anything special —
     just ship a model that looks right in those 4 angles. If your
     model has back-facing detail that only looks right from one
     angle, the validator's silhouette test will catch it.

  2. MULTI-VIEW IoU
     The renderer's `window.lblSpec.helpers.compareToReference(root,
     refImg)` (Python: `from js import lblSpec;
     lblSpec.helpers.compareToReference(root, refImg)`) computes
     the IoU between the rendered silhouette and the reference image.
     Use it for image-driven Python factories (see PART 254 / 280).

  3. TRIANGLE BUDGET
     The renderer's `meta.triBudget` field (default 8500 for chunky
     vehicles, 30000 for hero characters) is enforced by the
     lblValidateAgainstSpec() function. Python factories should set
     this explicitly:
         bp = lbl.blueprint()
         bp["meta"]["triBudget"] = 4500
         return bp
     Otherwise the default applies, and the validator may warn
     "over budget" or "under budget" depending on the model.

  4. BBOX OVERLAP
     PART 39.4 / E17 checks that no two meshes have overlapping
     bounding boxes unless they're meant to touch (e.g. body +
     leg). For Python factories, set `mesh.userData.attachedTo =
     "body"` on child meshes to declare the parent. The validator
     then only flags un-declared overlaps.

  5. HEIGHT MATCH
     The renderer's `meta.height` field declares the intended height
     in metres. The validator compares it to the resolved Y range of
     the geometry (per the PART 38.1 known issue in ANALYSIS_REPORT
     #2). Python factories should set both:
         bp["meta"]["height"] = 1.7
         # And ensure the geometry's Y range matches ±5%.

  6. CHEAT-SHEET CHECK
     The PART 152 reference cheat-sheet is the canonical 10-step ship
     list. For Python factories, the equivalent is PART 210 + PART
     214 (the pre-output checklist). Run through both silently
     before returning.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 249 — 10 NAMED PITFALLS WITH FIXES (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 151)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The 10 named pitfalls from PART 151, with Python-form fixes:

  P1.  STACKED CYLINDERS
       A limb built from 3-5 stacked CylinderGeometry meshes
       looks like a stack of coins.
       ✅ Use MT_core.loft(profile) — see PART 253.

  P2.  FLOATING JOINTS
       Eyeballed magic-number pivot offsets make arms/legs float.
       ✅ Derive offsets from the parent mesh's bounding box —
          see PART 251.

  P3.  SMOOTH-PLASTIC LOOK
       The default `MeshStandardMaterial` with no flatShading looks
       like smooth plastic, not chunky low-poly.
       ✅ Set `flatShading: True` on every material when the style
          is `chunky-mascot` / `flat-low-poly` / `voxel` — see
          PART 252.

  P4.  FLOATING PRIMITIVES
       Multiple separate primitives placed side-by-side leave
       visible gaps.
       ✅ Use `MT_core.sdfUnion` + `MT_core.sdfMarch` to merge
          contiguous parts into a single watertight mesh — see
          PART 250.

  P5.  MISSING TICKS
       A model that has visible per-frame motion (spinning wheels,
       waving antenna) but no `userData.tick` stays static.
       ✅ For Style B: `g.userData.tick = lambda dt, elapsed: ...`.
          For Style A: not supported — switch to Style B.

  P6.  WRONG SCALE
       A model that's 0.05 units tall is barely visible; one that's
       50 units tall overflows the viewport.
       ✅ Pick a convention: bbox should fit in roughly [-1, +1].
          See PART 228.2.

  P7.  MISSING CASTS / RECEIVES SHADOW
       A mesh with `castShadow = False` looks "floating" against
       a shadow-catcher floor.
       ✅ Set `castShadow = True` and `receiveShadow = True` on
          every body-part mesh.

  P8.  BAD PALETTE NAMING
       Palette entries named "color1", "color2", "color3" are
       unmaintainable.
       ✅ Use semantic names: `shell`, `accent`, `frame`,
          `darkMetal`, `glass`, `wheel`. The validator's PART
          48 audit surfaces non-semantic names as warnings.

  P9.  TOP-LEVEL SIDE EFFECTS
       Module-level `print()` / `random.seed()` / etc. fire on
       import, not on factory call. The renderer's reset-between-
       runs logic doesn't catch them.
       ✅ Wrap all setup in the factory function. The
          `mulberry32(seed)` helper from PART 209 is module-level
          OK because it's a pure function (no side effects).

  P10. FORGETTING .new() ON THREE.X CALLS
       `g = THREE.Group()` returns the class, not an instance.
       The renderer's `addTsModelToScene()` then chokes on the
       class object.
       ✅ Always use the Pyodide idiom: `g = THREE.Group.new()`.
          See PART 203.

================================================================================
PART 250-254 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: FLOATING-PARTS FIX PATCH
================================================================================

This is the Python adaptation of PART 250-254 in Prompt_To_Ts.txt
(the floating-parts fix patch). See that file for the canonical
methodology. PART 250-254 here is the Python-specific form, with
code snippets adapted to Pyodide's `from js import THREE` idiom.

The 5 rules apply identically to Python factories (because the
underlying renderer is the same). What changes is the helper
namespace: in Python, `MT_core` / `MT_meshes` etc. are accessed
via `from js import MT_core` (Pyodide bridges the global JS
namespace to Python), not `window.MT.*` (which is the TS path).

The IoU check in PART 254 has the same graceful-skip behaviour
in Python: if no reference image is available, the call is
silently bypassed via a `refImg` null-check, identical to the
TS path's behavior. Image_To_Py.txt ships a separate
Image_To_Py-specific IoU contract in PART 280.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 250 — PYTHON FORM: ENFORCE SDF + MARCHING CUBES FOR CONTIGUOUS PARTS
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 250)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  [THE RULE] (identical to TS PART 250)
  If a model has more than 2 contiguous body parts (head, torso,
  limbs, tail, etc.), the AI MUST use `MT_core.sdfUnion` and
  `MT_core.sdfMarch` to generate a single, watertight mesh.

  [PYTHON IMPLEMENTATION]
  ```python
  from js import MT_core, THREE  # Pyodide JS bridge

  # BAD: Separate primitives (causes floating parts)
  head = THREE.Mesh.new(THREE.SphereGeometry.new(0.2), mat)
  torso = THREE.Mesh.new(THREE.CylinderGeometry.new(0.2, 0.25, 0.5), mat)
  g.add(head); g.add(torso)  # visible gap at the neck!

  # GOOD: SDF Union (creates a unified, organic shape)
  sdf = MT_core.sdfUnion(
      MT_core.sdfSphere([0, 0.7, 0], 0.2),       # Head
      MT_core.sdfCapsule([0, 0.2, 0], [0, 0.6, 0], 0.2),  # Torso
  )
  body_geo = MT_core.sdfMarch(sdf, {"resolution": 64, "bound": 2})
  body_mesh = THREE.Mesh.new(body_geo, mat)
  g.add(body_mesh)  # single watertight mesh, no gap
  ```

  Note: `from js import MT_core` exposes the renderer's MT
  bundle to Python. If the bundle is unavailable (older
  renderer build), the Python factory falls back to the
  manual approach: a `THREE.SphereGeometry` + a `THREE.CapsuleGeometry`
  unioned via `THREE.BufferGeometryUtils.mergeGeometries()`.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 251 — PYTHON FORM: AUTOMATED PIVOT OFFSET CALCULATION
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 251)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  [THE RULE] (identical to TS PART 251)
  Pivot Offset MUST equal the distance from the parent's origin to
  its surface. No magic numbers.

  [PYTHON IMPLEMENTATION]
  ```python
  # BAD: Eyeballed magic number
  shoulder_L.position.set(-0.28, 0.1, 0)  # 0.28 came from where?

  # GOOD: Calculated from Bounding Box
  torso_geo = body_mesh.geometry
  torso_geo.computeBoundingBox()  # necessary before reading bbox
  bb = torso_geo.boundingBox
  torso_width = bb.max.x - bb.min.x  # full width, not half
  shoulder_L.position.set(-(torso_width * 0.5 * 0.9), 0.1, 0)
  # 0.5 → half-extent; 0.9 → 90% of half-width, leaving a 10% gap.
  # No magic numbers — derived from the actual mesh.
  ```

  Common gotcha: `geometry.boundingBox` is `None` until you call
  `.computeBoundingBox()`. Always call it before reading the bbox,
  or use `mesh.geometry.boundingSphere` (which is auto-computed
  on first read but less precise for box-like parts).

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 252 — PYTHON FORM: STRICT STYLE LOCK ENFORCEMENT
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 252)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  [THE RULE] (identical to TS PART 252)
  If the style lock is `chunky-mascot`, `flat-low-poly`, or `voxel`,
  every material MUST have `flatShading: True`. The look-dev pass
  MUST use `renderer.toneMapping = THREE.NoToneMapping`. An
  OutlinePass or screenspace outline MUST be generated.

  [PYTHON IMPLEMENTATION]
  ```python
  from js import THREE

  def make_chunky_material(color_hex, roughness=0.7, metalness=0.1):
      return THREE.MeshStandardMaterial.new({
          "color": color_hex,
          "roughness": roughness,
          "metalness": metalness,
          "flatShading": True,   # <-- the entire fix
      })

  # In the factory:
  shell_mat = make_chunky_material(0xcc2222, 0.7, 0.1)
  accent_mat = make_chunky_material(0xffd54f, 0.5, 0.05)

  # OutlinePass — the renderer's screen-space outline post-pass.
  # It's enabled by default for chunky-mascot models, so you
  # don't need to set it explicitly. If you want to disable it
  # (for a non-chunky style), set:
  #   g.userData.outlineEnabled = False
  # The renderer reads this in its lookDevLights() factory.

  # NoToneMapping — the renderer switches the renderer's
  # toneMapping automatically based on meta.style. For Style A,
  # set it via the returned dict:
  bp = lbl.blueprint()
  bp["meta"]["style"] = "chunky-mascot"  # auto-routes to NoToneMapping
  return bp
  ```

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 253 — PYTHON FORM: MANDATORY USE OF MT.core.loft FOR LIMBS
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 253)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  [THE RULE] (identical to TS PART 253)
  Limbs MUST be built via `MT_core.loft` with a cross-section
  profile, NOT stacked CylinderGeometry rings.

  [PYTHON IMPLEMENTATION]
  ```python
  from js import MT_core, THREE

  # BAD: Stacked cylinders (segmented look — looks like coins)
  for i in range(3):
      seg = THREE.Mesh.new(
          THREE.CylinderGeometry.new(0.08, 0.08, 0.08), mat_arm
      )
      seg.position.y = -0.1 - i * 0.1
      arm.add(seg)

  # GOOD: Single lofted mesh (continuous, natural taper)
  arm_profile = [
      {"t": 0.0, "radius": 0.10},  # Shoulder
      {"t": 0.5, "radius": 0.08},  # Elbow
      {"t": 1.0, "radius": 0.06},  # Wrist
  ]
  arm_geo = MT_core.loft(arm_profile, {"radial": 8, "length": 8})
  arm_mesh = THREE.Mesh.new(arm_geo, mat_arm)
  arm_pivot.add(arm_mesh)
  ```

  The `from js import MT_core` line is mandatory — without it,
  the `MT_core.loft()` call raises AttributeError at factory-run
  time, not import time. Add it at the top of every Python
  factory that uses any `MT_*` helper.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 254 — PYTHON FORM: SILHOUETTE IoU & VISUAL REGRESSION LOOP
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 254)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  [THE RULE] (identical to TS PART 254, with the same
              graceful-skip for prompt-only mode)

  [PYTHON IMPLEMENTATION — IMAGE-DRIVEN MODE]
  ```python
  from js import lblSpec

  def create_model():
      # ... build the model ...
      # The renderer exposes a refImg on the global lblSpec object
      # for image-driven factories. If you have one, run the IoU
      # check; if you don't, the call is silently bypassed.
      ref_img = lblSpec.refImg if hasattr(lblSpec, "refImg") else None
      if ref_img is not None:
          iou = lblSpec.helpers.compareToReference(g, ref_img)
          if iou < 0.80:
              # Scale up by 10% and retry once
              g.scale.set(1.1, 1.1, 1.1)
              iou = lblSpec.helpers.compareToReference(g, ref_img)
              if iou < 0.80:
                  print(f"Warning: IoU still {iou} after scale-up.")
      return g
  ```

  [PYTHON IMPLEMENTATION — PROMPT-ONLY MODE (graceful skip)]
  ```python
  # In prompt-only mode, lblSpec.refImg is None. The IoU
  # check is bypassed entirely. Don't error out — the
  # factory's job is to build the model, not to compare it
  # against a non-existent reference.

  def create_model():
      # ... build the model ...
      return g
  ```

  The same 0.80 threshold applies. If the model fails the
  check, the factory prints a warning to the validator log
  (Pyodide captures stdout — see PART 216.3) and returns
  anyway. The renderer's auto-frame on the Group keeps the
  model visible.

================================================================================
PART 255-259 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: HARD-SURFACE / VEHICLE PIPELINE
================================================================================

Python vehicle factories should follow the same 8-step Hard-Surface
/ Vehicle Modeling Pipeline as TS factories (Prompt_To_Ts.txt
PART 167-178). PART 255-259 is the Python adaptation. The
canonical methodology lives in Prompt_To_Ts.txt; this file ships
the Python form, with concrete `def` examples for the Python-
specific idioms (Pyodide + lbl.* + MT_highpoly).

The Chunky 3D Sport Motorcycle Reference Specification (PART 180-189
in Prompt_To_Ts.txt) is a worked example of the 8-step pipeline
applied to a chunky sport motorcycle. Python factories can mirror
it 1:1 with the `from js import THREE` idiom.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 255 — 8-STEP VEHICLE PIPELINE (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 167)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Step 1. PLAN             List the major sub-assemblies: chassis,
                            wheels, suspension, fairings, exhaust,
                            handlebar, seat, tank. For a sport
                            motorcycle: 8 sub-assemblies (PART 184
                            reference catalogue).

  Step 2. DIMENSIONS       Declare the dimension contract in
                            `meta.dimensions`:
                              bp = lbl.blueprint()
                              bp["meta"]["dimensions"] = {
                                  "wheelbase": 1.4,
                                  "track":     0.6,
                                  "ground_clearance": 0.18,
                                  "length":    2.1,
                                  "width":     0.8,
                                  "height":    1.2,
                              }
                              return bp
                            The validator (PART 168) checks plausibility.

  Step 3. WHEELS           Build wheels with the lathed-tire pattern
                            (PART 256.3 — `lbl.create_lathed_tire`).
                            Use a TorusGeometry + LatheGeometry for
                            the rim + tire, NOT a flat CylinderGeometry
                            (which fails the E41 distinct-Z test).

  Step 4. FAIRINGS         Use ExtrudeGeometry on a 2D Shape with
                            optional holes (vent cutouts, fairing
                            intakes). For a chunky 3D look, keep
                            the extrusion depth shallow (5-15 cm)
                            and the bevel small.

  Step 5. SUSPENSION       Build a kinematic chain:
                            chassis → swingarm_pivot → wheel_pivot.
                            Each pivot is a Group; the child is the
                            wheel mesh. The renderer's inspector
                            can rotate `swingarm_pivot.rotation`
                            interactively to test suspension travel.

  Step 6. LIGHTING         Add a 3-point rig: key + fill + rim.
                            Place them relative to the bike-forward
                            coordinate system (see PART 258.1).
                            Use `MT_lookdev.create_3point_rig(...)`.

  Step 7. AUDIT            Run the validator's PART 169-178 audits:
                            tri count, drawcall count, bbox, ground
                            clearance, attachment hierarchy.
                            Surface any BLOCK warnings.

  Step 8. SHIP             Save as a .py file with the canonical
                            `create_model` factory name. The
                            renderer's auto-validator picks it up
                            on drop.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 256 — 4 PROCEDURAL PRIMITIVE HELPERS (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 169)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The 4 procedural primitive helpers from PART 169, with their
Python forms:

  1. FILLETED BOX
     Original TS:
         const fb = MT_meshes.filletedBox(w, h, d, r)
     Python:
         from js import MT_meshes
         fb_geo = MT_meshes.filletedBox(w, h, d, r)  # returns BufferGeometry

  2. TAPERED TUBE
     TS:
         const tt = MT_meshes.taperedTube(profile, opts)
     Python:
         tt_geo = MT_meshes.taperedTube(profile, opts)
     Useful for swingarms, fork tubes, exhaust headers.

  3. RIM STAR PATTERN
     TS:
         const rs = MT_meshes.rimStarPattern(radius, spokes, opts)
     Python:
         rs_geo = MT_meshes.rimStarPattern(radius, spokes, opts)
     5-spoke alloy rims (the PART 184 canonical pattern).

  4. BEVELED WASHER
     TS:
         const bw = MT_meshes.beveledWasher(innerR, outerR, thickness, bevel)
     Python:
         bw_geo = MT_meshes.beveledWasher(innerR, outerR, thickness, bevel)
     For bolt heads, fairing mounts, exhaust clamps.

  256.3 — LATHED TIRE (the canonical wheel-tire pattern)
    Original TS (PART 179.1):
        const tire = createLathedTireGeometry({
            outerR: 0.32, innerR: 0.18, width: 0.18,
            treadDepth: 0.012, sidewallStyle: "chunky",
        })
    Python:
        from js import MT_hardsurface
        tire_geo = MT_hardsurface.createLathedTireGeometry({
            "outerR": 0.32,
            "innerR": 0.18,
            "width": 0.18,
            "treadDepth": 0.012,
            "sidewallStyle": "chunky",
        })
    The `sidewallStyle: "chunky"` option is the canonical PART 180
    setting for the Sport Motorcycle archetype.

  256.4 — CATMULL-ROM SWEPT TUBE (exhaust headers, frame tubes)
    Python:
        from js import MT_hardsurface
        header_geo = MT_hardsurface.createSweptTube(
            control_points,  # list of [x, y, z] waypoints
            {"radius": 0.022, "segments": 64, "twist": False},
        )
    See PART 179.4 for the full pattern.

  256.5 — BEZIER EXTRUDE FAIRING
    Python:
        from js import MT_hardsurface
        fairing_geo = MT_hardsurface.createBezierExtrude(
            shape,            # THREE.Shape with optional holes
            {"depth": 0.15, "bevelSize": 0.012, "bevelThickness": 0.012,
             "curveSegments": 12},
        )
    Used for the Sport Motorcycle's side fairing, tank shell, and
    front cowl.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 257 — 5 FAIRING TECHNIQUES (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 170)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The 5 fairing techniques from PART 170, with Python forms:

  1. QUADRATIC          A simple quadratic curve in 2D, then
                        ExtrudeGeometry on the resulting Shape.
                        Python:
                            from js import THREE
                            shape = THREE.Shape.new()
                            shape.moveTo(0, 0)
                            shape.quadraticCurveTo(0.5, 0.3, 1.0, 0)
                            shape.lineTo(1.0, 0.3)
                            shape.lineTo(0, 0.3)
                            shape.closePath()
                            geo = THREE.ExtrudeGeometry.new(shape, {"depth": 0.1})

  2. BEZIER             Cubic Bezier in 2D. The shape.holes
                        property takes THREE.Path objects.
                        Python:
                            from js import THREE
                            path = THREE.Path.new()
                            path.moveTo(0.1, 0.1)
                            path.bezierCurveTo(0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 0.5, 0.9, 0.1)
                            shape.holes.push(path)
                            geo = THREE.ExtrudeGeometry.new(shape, opts)

  3. LATHE              For rotationally symmetric parts (wheel
                        hubs, headlight bezels, exhaust tips):
                        Python:
                            from js import THREE
                            points = [[0.0, 0.0], [0.05, 0.0], [0.05, 0.08],
                                     [0.04, 0.09], [0.0, 0.09]]
                            geo = THREE.LatheGeometry.new(points, 32)

  4. SWEPT EXTRUSION    For tubes / bars / exhaust headers:
                        PART 256.4 above.

  5. PARAMETRIC         For complex fairing surfaces (a bike
                        fairing with multiple compound curves):
                        Python:
                            from js import MT_hardsurface
                            # buildFairing(opts) — uses bezier
                            # surface + lathe + boolean union
                            # to create a chunky fairing shape.
                            fairing_geo = MT_hardsurface.buildFairing({
                                "frontShape": "wedge",
                                "sideShape": "scoop",
                                "depth": 0.4,
                                "bevel": 0.02,
                            })

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 258 — SEEDED MICRO-ROUGHNESS TEXTURE (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 171)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

Python factories should use the same Mulberry32 seeded micro-
roughness texture as TS factories, for deterministic procedural
detail. The Python helper:

      from js import THREE

      def make_micro_roughness_texture(seed=1, size=256):
          # Mulberry32 PRNG — same as TS PART 8.1
          state = [seed & 0xFFFFFFFF]
          def rand():
              state[0] = (state[0] + 0x6D2B79F5) & 0xFFFFFFFF
              t = state[0]
              t = ((t ^ (t >> 15)) * (t | 1)) & 0xFFFFFFFF
              t = (t ^ (t + (((t ^ (t >> 7)) * (t | 61)) & 0xFFFFFFFF))) & 0xFFFFFFFF
              return ((t ^ (t >> 14)) & 0xFFFFFFFF) / 4294967296

          canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
          canvas.width = size
          canvas.height = size
          ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
          # 4x4 random gray cells, RepeatWrapping for tiling
          cell = size // 4
          for y in range(4):
              for x in range(4):
                  v = int(rand() * 256)
                  ctx.fillStyle = f"rgb({v},{v},{v})"
                  ctx.fillRect(x * cell, y * cell, cell, cell)
          tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)
          tex.wrapS = THREE.RepeatWrapping
          tex.wrapT = THREE.RepeatWrapping
          tex.repeat.set(4, 4)
          return tex

  Apply to a material:
      mat = THREE.MeshStandardMaterial.new({
          "color": 0xf5821b,
          "roughness": 0.55,
          "roughnessMap": make_micro_roughness_texture(seed=42),
      })

  For high-poly PBR surfaces, the `lbl.photogrammetry_pbr()`
  recipe (PART 239) is preferred — it has the normalMap response
  baked in.

  258.1 — BIKE-FORWARD COORDINATE SYSTEM
    Sport motorcycle models in Python should use the bike-forward
    coordinate system from PART 185:
      +X = front
      -X = rear
      +Y = up
      +Z = right (rider's right)
    Origin (0, 0, 0) is between the axles, on the ground.
    Axles at Y ≈ 0.30 (so the lathed tires touch ground at Y = 0).
    This matches the TS coordinate system exactly, so a Python
    motorcycle and a TS motorcycle of the same dimensions are
    interchangeable on the page.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 259 — 12-STEP SHIP CHECKLIST (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 174-178)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The 12-step ship checklist from PART 174, with Python-form checks:

  1.  [ ] Plan + dimensions declared in `meta.dimensions` (PART 255.2)
  2.  [ ] Wheels use lathed-tire pattern, not flat cylinder
       (PART 256.3)
  3.  [ ] Fairings use ExtrudeGeometry or lathe, not boxes
       (PART 257)
  4.  [ ] Suspension pivots form a kinematic chain (PART 255.5)
  5.  [ ] Materials use the 6-band reference palette (PART 183
       reference) with semantic names
  6.  [ ] 3-point lighting rig added via `MT_lookdev.create_3point_rig`
  7.  [ ] castShadow + receiveShadow set on every body-part mesh
  8.  [ ] sculptor_runtime nodes/meshes populated (PART 207)
  9.  [ ] detailInventory matches the PART 184 detail catalog
  10. [ ] Triangle count within ±15% of target (~8500 for chunky
        motorcycle, see PART 188)
  11. [ ] Drawcall count within budget (~50 for chunky motorcycle)
  12. [ ] lblValidateAgainstSpec() shows 0 BLOCK warnings

  The last step is automated — the renderer runs the validator
  on drop. The Python factory's job is to make sure steps 1-11
  pass silently.

================================================================================
PART 260-264 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: ARTIST-FIRST GEOMETRY FLOW
================================================================================

Python factories should follow the same Artist-First Geometry Flow
as TS factories (PART 179 in Prompt_To_Ts.txt). PART 260-264 is the
Python adaptation. The "Hierarchy of Fidelity" rule applies
identically: visual silhouette > compound contour > PBR tactile
material > mechanical skeleton > code minification.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 260 — HIERARCHY OF FIDELITY (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 179)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

When two rules conflict, the higher-priority one wins. Python
factories must respect this order:

  1. VISUAL SILHOUETTE   The 4-angle silhouette test (PART 248.1)
                         beats everything below. If the model
                         doesn't read as a [subject] from front /
                         side / top / 3/4, no amount of detail fixes
                         it.

  2. COMPOUND CONTOUR    The compound contour (the way multiple
                         primitives blend into a single mass)
                         beats material choice. PART 250 (SDF +
                         Marching Cubes) is the methodology for
                         making compound contours; the Python
                         form is identical to the TS form.

  3. PBR TACTILE MATERIAL The choice of roughness / metalness /
                         normal map beats the underlying geometry.
                         A well-textured box can look better
                         than a poorly-textured organic shape.

  4. MECHANICAL SKELETON  The rig / pivot hierarchy / naming
                         convention beats polygon count. A
                         well-named, well-pivoted model with
                         1000 tris beats a poorly-named one
                         with 5000 tris.

  5. CODE MINIFICATION   The factory's file size is LAST. Don't
                         sacrifice readability to save 200
                         characters.

  260.1 — RULE 167.1.1 FORM PRESERVATION MANDATE (PYTHON)
    When porting a TS vehicle factory to Python, the form must
    be preserved. Don't replace ExtrudeGeometry fairings with
    BoxGeometry (it'll look like a flat slab) just because
    BoxGeometry is shorter. The form is the language; Python
    is just a different syntax for the same shapes.

  260.2 — RULE 169.1.2 FILLETED BOX USAGE CONSTRAINT (PYTHON)
    The `MT_meshes.filletedBox(...)` helper (PART 256.1) is
    PROHIBITED for fairings / cowls / tanks / visors / tires
    in the Sport Motorcycle archetype. Use ExtrudeGeometry
    for fairings and lathed tire for tires. FilletedBox is
    only for STRUCTURAL members (frame spars, brackets,
    mounting plates).

  260.3 — RULE 169.3.2 MANDATORY WHEEL PROFILE REVOLUTION (PYTHON)
    All wheels in Python vehicle factories MUST use a revolved
    profile (lathed tire from PART 256.3, or LatheGeometry
    for a custom profile). A flat CylinderGeometry wheel
    fails the E41 distinct-Z test.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 261 — FORM-PRESERVATION RECIPES (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 179.1-179.10)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The 10 form-preservation recipes from PART 179.1-179.10, with
Python forms:

  179.1  LATHED TIRE        — MT_hardsurface.createLathedTireGeometry
  179.2  (same as 179.1)
  179.3  SWEPT TUBE         — MT_hardsurface.createSweptTube
  179.4  EXHAUST CANISTER   — MT_hardsurface.createExhaustCanister
  179.5  BEZIER EXTRUDE     — MT_hardsurface.createBezierExtrude
  179.6  SHAPE-HOLE DUCT    — THREE.Shape + .holes (no helper needed)
  179.7  EGG-VISOR FIX      — Use LatheGeometry on a half-ellipse
  179.8  TS1117 FIX         — The lbl helper (PART 202) resolves
                              duplicate-key conflicts in Style A
                              factories.
  179.9  SOCKET ATTACHMENT  — Use lbl.create_anchor() for modular
        HIERARCHY             attach points.
  179.10 FORM-PRESERVATION  — Run lblValidateAgainstSpec() before
        SHIP-LIST             returning; surface any BLOCK warnings.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 262 — PROCEDURAL HELPERS (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 179)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The 3 new procedural helpers from PART 179, in Python form:

  1. createLathedTireGeometry(opts)
     Python:
         from js import MT_hardsurface
         tire_geo = MT_hardsurface.createLathedTireGeometry(opts)

  2. createSweptTube(control_points, opts)
     Python:
         from js import MT_hardsurface
         tube_geo = MT_hardsurface.createSweptTube(
             [[0,0,0], [0.1, 0.05, 0], [0.2, 0.0, 0.1]],
             {"radius": 0.022, "segments": 64, "twist": False},
         )

  3. createExhaustCanister(opts)
     Python:
         from js import MT_hardsurface
         exhaust_geo = MT_hardsurface.createExhaustCanister({
             "length": 0.4,
             "ovalRadius": [0.06, 0.045],  # [X, Y] radii
             "endcapStyle": "stepped",
         })

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 263 — TS1117 DUPLICATE-KEY FIX (PYTHON)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 179.8)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The TS1117 "Duplicate identifier" error happens when a TS factory
imports the same name from two different modules. The Python
equivalent is `ImportError: cannot import name 'X' from 'js'`,
which fires when the renderer hasn't mirrored the class yet.

Fix in Python: ensure the lbl helper module is loaded BEFORE
your factory runs. The renderer does this automatically, but if
you're running Pyodide in a custom environment, call:

    import lbl  # forces the module to load
    # Now from js import MT_core works
    from js import MT_core

The same fix applies to `MT_hardsurface`, `MT_architectural`,
`MT_highpoly`, `MT_textures`. Import lbl first.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 264 — STYLE-AGNOSTICISM (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 38.22)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The system is style-agnostic — 13 canonical styles, including
`pbr-realistic` and `photogrammetry`. Python factories opt into
a style via:

  Style A (Blueprint-Return):
      bp = lbl.blueprint()
      bp["meta"]["style"] = "pbr-realistic"  # or any canonical
      return bp

  Style B (Three.js Direct):
      g.userData.meta = g.userData.meta or {}
      g.userData.meta["style"] = "pbr-realistic"
      return g

The renderer's lookDevLights() factory (PART 187 / 199.3 mirror)
reads `meta.style` and switches the lighting rig, the tone
mapping, the post-processing pass, and the triangle budget
guidance (PART 50.4) accordingly. The default is `smooth-low-poly`
(matches the project name and the demo models). Override
explicitly if your model is in a different style.

================================================================================
PART 265-269 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: SPORT MOTORCYCLE REFERENCE
================================================================================

Python factories that produce chunky-mascot sport motorcycles
should follow PART 180-189 (Prompt_To_Ts.txt) one-for-one. The
SportMotorcycle_3D_Techniques_Specification.txt at repo root is
the canonical reference; PART 180-189 codifies it as 10 PARTs.
PART 265-269 here is the Python form, with the lbl.* / MT_*
helpers explicit.

For most Python factories, you don't need a full motorcycle
build — only the relevant sub-sections. The PART 270-274 worked
example for chunky-mascot vehicles (a small scooter, not a full
sport bike) is the right starting point for most use cases.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 265 — 8 BUILDER FUNCTIONS (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 184)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

The 8 builder functions from PART 184, with Python forms:

  1. createChunkySportWheel(opts)        — the wheel + lathed tire
  2. buildFrontForkAssembly(opts)        — fork tubes + triple clamp
  3. buildFairingAndCockpit(opts)        — fairing + windshield +
                                           headlight + mirrors
  4. buildFuelTank(opts)                 — tank + cap
  5. buildSeatAndTailSection(opts)       — seat + tail + license plate
  6. buildChassisAndEngine(opts)         — frame + engine block +
                                           cylinder heads
  7. buildExhaustSystem(opts)            — header pipes + canister +
                                           tip
  8. buildRearSwingarm(opts)             — swingarm + shock + pivot

  Each builder returns a dict: `{"group": Group, "meshes": {...},
  "sockets": {...}, "colliders": {...}}`. This is the same shape
  as the TS builders, just with snake_case keys.

  Python convention (snake_case throughout):
      from js import THREE, MT_hardsurface

      def build_chassis_and_engine(opts):
          g = THREE.Group.new()
          g.name = "chassis"
          # ... build the chassis ...
          return {
              "group": g,
              "meshes": {"frame_spar_L": frame_spar_L, ...},
              "sockets": {"engine_mount": engine_mount_socket},
          }

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 266 — 9-KEY sculptRuntime CONTRACT (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 186)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 9-key contract is the same as TS:

      g.userData.sculptRuntime = {
          "nodes":             {...},  # name → Group/Mesh
          "meshes":            {...},  # name → Mesh
          "sockets":           {...},  # name → Anchor
          "colliders":         {...},  # name → Box/Sphere/Capsule collider
          "destructionGroups": {...},  # name → list of mesh names
          "materials":         {...},  # name → THREE.Material
          "actions":           {...},  # name → callable (Actions API)
          "animation":         {...},  # AnimationClips + mixer
          "vfx":               {...},  # name → particle/light/effect handle
      }

  5 Actions API methods (Python form):

      def rev_engine(self, intensity=1.0):
          self.userData.sculptRuntime["animation"]["engine_rpm"] = (
              800 + intensity * 7000
          )

      def steer(self, angle):
          self.userData.sculptRuntime["animation"]["steering_angle"] = angle

      def set_drive_speed(self, speed):
          self.userData.sculptRuntime["animation"]["drive_speed"] = speed

      def toggle_headlight(self):
          self.userData.sculptRuntime["vfx"]["headlight_on"] = (
              not self.userData.sculptRuntime["vfx"]["headlight_on"]
          )

      def reset_pose(self):
          for k, v in self.userData.sculptRuntime["animation"].items():
              if isinstance(v, (int, float)):
                  self.userData.sculptRuntime["animation"][k] = 0

  Per-frame tick:
      def tick(self, dt, elapsed):
          anim = self.userData.sculptRuntime["animation"]
          # Spin wheels based on drive_speed
          if "drive_speed" in anim:
              speed = anim["drive_speed"]
              self.userData.sculptRuntime["nodes"]["wheel_front"].rotation.x += (
                  dt * speed * 5.0
              )
              self.userData.sculptRuntime["nodes"]["wheel_rear"].rotation.x += (
                  dt * speed * 5.0
              )
          # Steering rotation
          if "steering_angle" in anim:
              angle = anim["steering_angle"]
              self.userData.sculptRuntime["nodes"]["fork_pivot"].rotation.y = (
                  angle
              )

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 267 — LOOK-DEV LIGHTING FACTORY (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 187)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 3-mode lighting factory in Python:

      from js import MT_lookdev

      def create_sport_motorcycle_look_dev_lights(mode):
          return MT_lookdev.createSportMotorcycleModelLookDevLights(mode)

  3 modes:
      - "reference" — 3-point studio rig + soft shadow (PART 187 default)
      - "grazing"   — 1 strong rim + dark fill (for showing form)
      - "neutral"   — even ambient + low-contrast key (for screenshots)

  2048 shadow map contract — the renderer's renderer.shadowMap.size
  is set to 2048 for these models. Override via:
      g.userData.shadowMapSize = 4096  # for hero shots

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 268 — TRIANGLE BUDGET + 8 ARCHITECTURAL PATTERNS (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 188)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  ~8500 tris is the target for a chunky sport motorcycle. The
  Python factory sets this via:

      bp = lbl.blueprint()
      bp["meta"]["triBudget"] = 8500
      return bp

  Or, for Style B:
      g.userData.meta = g.userData.meta or {}
      g.userData.meta["triBudget"] = 8500

  The renderer's lblValidateAgainstSpec() surfaces a warning if
  the model is >15% over or under budget.

  The 8 architectural patterns from PART 188 (factory, options
  object, runtime contract, modular builder, named registry,
  action/command, deterministic procedural, zero-dependency
  standalone) all apply identically to Python. They're
  language-neutral patterns, not language-specific idioms.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 269 — 5-FIELD STYLE + LBL VERSION STAMP (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 189)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  Every Python factory that produces a chunky 3D archetype
  (sport motorcycle, scooter, etc.) should set the 5-field
  pair on meta:

      bp = lbl.blueprint()
      bp["meta"]["style"]           = "chunky-mascot"
      bp["meta"]["subject"]         = "sport-motorcycle"  # or scooter, etc.
      bp["meta"]["sourceReference"] = "bd5da92411032501f007edb073f06372.jpg"
      bp["meta"]["referenceViews"]  = "isometric-front-right"
      bp["meta"]["lblVersion"]      = "v1.36 / v8.27"
      return bp

  The dual-version stamp rule (TS v1.x paired with JSON-JS v8.x,
  always released together) is canonical and applies to all 4
  factory formats (JSON / JS / TS / Python). The renderer reads
  meta.lblVersion to verify the factory is targeting the right
  pair.

================================================================================
PART 270-274 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: ARCHITECTURAL MODELING
================================================================================

Python factories that produce buildings, interiors, or landscape
elements should follow PART 190-199 (Prompt_To_Ts.txt). The
canonical methodology lives in Prompt_To_Ts.txt; PART 270-274 here
is the Python form, with the lbl_architectural helper module
explicit.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 270 — 6 ARCHITECTURAL PITFALLS (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 190)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 6 pitfalls from PART 190, with Python-specific notes:

  1. CSG VOID             When Boolean operations fail, the
                          resulting mesh has invisible voids.
                          In Python, the renderer's
                          `MT_architectural.createWallWithApertures`
                          (PART 271.1) avoids this by using
                          piecewise sub-wall decomposition
                          instead of CSG.

  2. 2D→3D DEPTH          A 2D shape extruded with depth=0
                          collapses to a 2D plane (invisible
     MISMATCH              from some angles). Always set
                          `bevelSize` and `bevelThickness`
                          to a non-zero value (e.g. 0.012)
                          in the ExtrudeGeometry opts.

  3. PIVOT DRIFT          Eyeballed magic-number pivots
                          (PART 251 rule) cause misalignment.
                          In Python, always use the bbox-
                          derived offset pattern (PART 251.1).

  4. SCHEMA NAMING        Inconsistent naming (`wall_1`,
                          `Wall01`, `theWall`) breaks the
                          sculptRuntime's "find by name" API.
                          Use snake_case + semantic: `wall_N`,
                          `wall_S`, `wall_E`, `wall_W` for
                          the 4 walls of a building.

  5. COPLANAR Z-FIGHTING  Two surfaces at exactly the same
                          depth z-fight (the "shimmering"
                          visual artifact). The renderer's
                          STACK_INDEX (PART 191) is the
                          Python-visible solution:

                              from js import MT_architectural
                              STACK = MT_architectural.STACK_INDEX
                              # In a facade builder:
                              wall_geo.translate(0, 0, STACK["WALL_FACING"])
                              # 0.001m offset prevents z-fight.

  6. MONOLITHIC CODE      A 5000-line single .py file is hard
                          to maintain. Python's natural
     VOLUME               module structure is the solution —
                          split the factory into a top-level
                          `create_model` that imports helper
                          builders from sibling files (e.g.
                          `from wall_builder import build_walls`).
                          The renderer's Python module loader
                          handles sibling imports.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 271 — 7 PARAMETRIC CONSTRUCTORS (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 192-198)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 7 parametric constructors, with Python forms:

  1. createWallWithApertures(opts)   — `MT_architectural.createWallWithApertures`
  2. createHingedDoorUnit(opts)       — `MT_architectural.createHingedDoorUnit`
  3. createGlazedWindowUnit(opts)     — `MT_architectural.createGlazedWindowUnit`
  4. createPitchedGableRoof(opts)     — `MT_architectural.createPitchedGableRoof`
  5. createLouveredAtticVent(opts)    — `MT_architectural.createLouveredAtticVent`
  6. createPottedPlant(opts)          — `MT_architectural.createPottedPlant`
  7. createSteppingStonePath(opts)    — `MT_architectural.createSteppingStonePath`

  Each takes a Python dict of opts. The opts schema matches
  the TS form (see PART 192-198 in Prompt_To_Ts.txt for the
  full reference).

  Example — a 4-wall house with door + 2 windows:

      from js import MT_architectural, THREE

      def build_house(opts):
          house = THREE.Group.new()
          house.name = "house"
          # 4 walls
          for i, side in enumerate(("N", "E", "S", "W")):
              wall_geo = MT_architectural.createWallWithApertures({
                  "width": opts.get("wall_length", 4.0),
                  "height": opts.get("wall_height", 2.8),
                  "thickness": 0.15,
                  "apertures": (
                      [{"type": "door", "x": 0.5, "width": 0.9, "height": 2.1}]
                      if side == "S" else
                      [{"type": "window", "x": 1.5, "width": 0.8, "height": 0.8},
                       {"type": "window", "x": 2.7, "width": 0.8, "height": 0.8}]
                      if side == "N" else []
                  ),
              })
              wall_mesh = THREE.Mesh.new(wall_geo, opts["material_wall"])
              wall_mesh.rotation.y = i * (math.pi / 2)
              wall_mesh.name = f"wall_{side}"
              house.add(wall_mesh)
          # Gable roof
          roof_geo = MT_architectural.createPitchedGableRoof({
              "width": opts.get("wall_length", 4.0) + 0.3,
              "length": opts.get("wall_length", 4.0) + 0.3,
              "pitch": 30,  # degrees
              "tileRows": 8,
          })
          roof_mesh = THREE.Mesh.new(roof_geo, opts["material_roof"])
          roof_mesh.position.y = opts.get("wall_height", 2.8)
          roof_mesh.name = "roof"
          house.add(roof_mesh)
          return house

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 272 — STACK_INDEX ANTI-Z-FIGHTING (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 191)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 11-key STACK_INDEX is exposed to Python via the
  `MT_architectural.STACK_INDEX` dict:

      from js import MT_architectural
      STACK = MT_architectural.STACK_INDEX
      # STACK = {
      #   "FOUNDATION":  0.000,
      #   "GRAVEL":      0.001,
      #   "PLAINTH":     0.002,
      #   "PORCH_DECK":  0.003,
      #   "DOOR_SILL":   0.004,
      #   "PAVERS":      0.005,
      #   "WALL_FACE":   0.010,
      #   "WALL_FACING": 0.011,  # <-- PART 270.5
      #   "WALL_TRIM":   0.012,
      #   "HARDWARE":    0.020,
      #   "GLASS":       0.030,
      # }

  Apply with `mesh.position.z += STACK["WALL_FACING"]` after
  building each layer. The renderer's `MT_architectural` module
  does this automatically in the parametric constructors, so you
  usually don't need to do it manually — but the dict is exposed
  for custom architectural elements.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 273 — 4-MODE LOOK-DEV LIGHTING (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 199.3)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  Python form:

      from js import MT_architectural

      lights = MT_architectural.createArchitecturalLookDevLights(mode)
      # mode = "day" | "dusk" | "night" | "warmglow"

  Each mode returns a 3-point rig tuned for that time of day:
      - day:        bright key + cool fill + warm rim (default)
      - dusk:       warm key + purple fill + cool rim
      - night:      dim cool key + warm interior rim + dark
      - warmglow:   single warm key + heavy orange fill

  Apply to the scene:
      scene = lblSpec.scene  # the renderer's THREE.Scene
      for light in lights["lights"]:
          scene.add(light)
      if "shadowCatcher" in lights:
          scene.add(lights["shadowCatcher"])

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 274 — 9-KEY MATERIAL PALETTE (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 199.4)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 9-key architectural palette is exposed via:

      from js import MT_architectural
      palette = MT_architectural.materialPalette()
      # palette = {
      #   "wall_face":    {"color": "#e8e0d4", "roughness": 0.85},
      #   "wall_facing":  {"color": "#c8b8a0", "roughness": 0.7},
      #   "wall_trim":    {"color": "#5a4a3a", "roughness": 0.5},
      #   "roof_tile":    {"color": "#7a3a2a", "roughness": 0.65},
      #   "door":         {"color": "#3a2a1a", "roughness": 0.55},
      #   "window_glass": {"color": "#aef5ff", "roughness": 0.05,
      #                    "metalness": 0.0, "transparent": True,
      #                    "opacity": 0.7},
      #   "hardware":     {"color": "#3a3a3a", "roughness": 0.3,
      #                    "metalness": 0.85},
      #   "porch_deck":   {"color": "#8a6a4a", "roughness": 0.8},
      #   "foundation":   {"color": "#a0a0a0", "roughness": 0.95},
      # }

  Use as a starting point in `lbl.define_palette()`:

      from js import MT_architectural
      palette = MT_architectural.materialPalette()
      lbl.define_palette(palette)
      # Now any `material="wall_face"` reference resolves.

  Or override individual entries:
      palette["wall_face"]["color"] = "#f4e8d4"  # lighter
      lbl.define_palette(palette)

  The renderer's lblValidateAgainstSpec() accepts the 9-key
  palette as canonical for architectural models. Adding extra
  keys is allowed; removing any of the 9 is a PART 199.4 warning.

================================================================================
PART 281-285 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: 44-TECHNIQUE MODELING BUNDLE
================================================================================

The renderer ships a 44-helper MT bundle (PART 100-143 in
Prompt_To_Ts.txt / Image_To_Ts.txt) on `window.MT_core` /
`MT_mesh` / `MT_materials` / `MT_organic` / `MT_advanced`. The TS
specs document each helper in detail. PART 281-285 here is the
Python adaptation: each of the 44 helpers gets a Python example
showing the `from js import MT_*` idiom and the kwargs shape.

This is the missing layer the original v1.36 / v8.27 release
needed — before this addition, Python factories referenced the
MT bundle as a group ("use `from js import MT_core`") but didn't
know what each helper did or how to call it. With PART 281-285,
every helper is callable from Python with a documented signature.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 281 — MT.core (12 CORE HELPERS, PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 100-111)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

The 12 CORE helpers, with Python forms. All accessed via
`from js import MT_core` (the Pyodide bridge to the renderer's
window.MT_core).

  1. PRIMITIVE
        from js import MT_core
        geo = MT_core.makePrimitive("box", {"w": 1, "h": 1, "d": 1})
        # kinds: "box" | "sphere" | "cylinder" | "cone" |
        #        "torus" | "icosahedron" | "plane" | "capsule"
        # Returns BufferGeometry.

  2. BMESH
        geo = MT_core.bmeshFromGeometry(geom)
        # Returns a BMesh object. Use MT_core.bmeshOp(bm, "extrude")
        # / "subdivide" / "bevel" / "inset" for editing ops.
        # Python mirrors the TS BMesh API.

  3. PROCEDURAL
        geo = MT_core.proceduralGenerate("F", {"F": "F[+F]F[-F]F"},
                                          {"iterations": 4})
        # L-system-style grammar. Returns a BufferGeometry built
        # by interpreting the generated string as turtle commands.

  4. PARAMETRIC
        geo = MT_core.makeParametric(
            lambda u, v: [u, v, 0],        # x(u,v)
            lambda u, v: [0, 0, 1],        # y(u,v) (normal)
            {"uSegments": 32, "vSegments": 32},
        )
        # Parametric surface from u,v → position function. Used
        # for mathematical surfaces (a sphere, a torus, a
        # Möbius strip, etc.).

  5. GENERATIVE
        geo = MT_core.generativeGrow({
            "iterations": 50,
            "stepLength": 0.05,
            "bias": [0, 1, 0],   # upward bias
            "fitness": lambda pos: pos[1] < 2.0,
            "branchProb": 0.2,
            "seed": 1,
        })
        # L-system + space colonization. Used for trees, roots,
        # branching networks.

  6. SDF
        sdf = MT_core.sdfUnion(
            MT_core.sdfSphere([0, 0, 0], 1.0),
            MT_core.sdfCapsule([0, 1, 0], [0, 2, 0], 0.5),
        )
        geo = MT_core.sdfMarch(sdf, {"resolution": 64, "bound": 2})
        # Signed distance field. See PART 250 for the full
        # floating-parts fix pattern.

  7. VOXEL
        vg = MT_core.voxelCarve(geom, {"resolution": 32})
        geo = MT_core.marchingCubesFromVoxels(vg)
        # Convert any geometry to a voxel grid, then back to a
        # marching-cubes mesh. Used for minecraft-style blocky
        # output and high-detail voxel art.

  8. CSG (Boolean)
        from js import MT_core
        geo = MT_core.csgUnion(mesh_a, mesh_b)
        # Or csgSubtract / csgIntersect. Requires three-bvh-csg
        # to be loaded (the renderer auto-loads it). See PART 244.1
        # for the Pyodide-specific gotchas.

  9. L-SYSTEM
        geo = MT_core.lSystem(
            "F",                                  # axiom
            {"F": "F[+F]F[-F]F"},                # rules
            4,                                    # iterations
        )
        # Or lSystemInterpret(str, opts) to interpret a string
        # as turtle commands. Both are exposed in PART 100-143
        # and the procedural-generate helper above.

  10. FRACTAL
        geo = MT_core.fractalMandelbulb({
            "power": 8,
            "iterations": 12,
            "resolution": 64,
        })
        # Or fractalMenger / fractalRaymarch. The renderer ships
        # 3 fractal kinds. Use for procedural abstract art.

  11. NURBS
        curve = MT_core.makeNurbsCurve(
            [[0, 0, 0], [1, 1, 0], [2, 0, 0], [3, -1, 0]],
            3,        # degree
            None,     # knots (auto-uniform if None)
        )
        # Or makeCatmullRom(points, opts). Used for smooth
        # curves and swept-tube geometry (PART 256.4).

  12. NON-MANIFOLD
        is_valid = MT_core.detectNonManifold(geom)
        # Returns True/False. The renderer runs this check on
        # every imported mesh (PART 235 importExternalMesh).
        fixed_geom = MT_core.fixNonManifold(geom)
        # Auto-fixes common non-manifold cases (duplicate verts,
        # open edges, etc.). Use after CSG operations.

  Use pattern (Style B):
        from js import THREE, MT_core

        def create_model():
            g = THREE.Group.new()
            # Make a CSG-subtracted gear
            outer = MT_core.makePrimitive("cylinder", {"rT": 1, "rB": 1, "h": 0.2})
            inner = MT_core.makePrimitive("cylinder", {"rT": 0.7, "rB": 0.7, "h": 0.3})
            gear_geo = MT_core.csgSubtract(outer, inner)
            gear_mesh = THREE.Mesh.new(gear_geo, mat_gear)
            g.add(gear_mesh)
            return g

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 282 — MT.mesh (9 MESH QUALITY HELPERS, PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 112-120)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  13. SUBDIVISION
        from js import MT_mesh
        new_geo = MT_mesh.subdivideCatmullClark(geom, {"levels": 2})
        # Or subdivideLoop. ~4× verts per level. Use to boost
        # low-poly → high-poly for smooth shading (PART 240).

  14. REMESHING
        new_geo = MT_mesh.isotropicRemesh(geom, {
            "targetEdgeLength": 0.05,
            "iterations": 5,
        })
        # Even triangle distribution. Use to fix bad topology
        # from imported meshes (PART 235).

  15. RETOPOLOGY
        new_geo = MT_mesh.autoRetopologize(geom)
        # Auto-generate a clean quad mesh from a messy input.
        # Used for high-poly → low-poly decimation (PART 241).

  16. DECIMATION
        new_geo = MT_mesh.qemDecimate(geom, {"ratio": 0.3})
        # Quadric Error Metric decimation. Keep 30% of triangles.
        # Preserves silhouette + topology. Use for high-poly →
        # low-poly conversion (PART 241).

  17. TRIANGULATION
        new_geo = MT_mesh.triangulate(polygon, indices)
        # Convert a polygon (n-gon) into triangles via ear
        # clipping. Use after shape-holes (PART 179.6) to
        # get a clean triangulated mesh.

  18. QUAD CONVERSION
        new_geo = MT_mesh.quadify(geom, {"angleThreshold": 0.5})
        # Convert triangles to quads where the dihedral angle
        # is below the threshold. Used for clean character
        # topology (PART 30).

  19. SMOOTHING
        new_geo = MT_mesh.laplacianSmooth(geom, {"iterations": 3})
        # Or taubinSmooth (which preserves volume better).
        # Use to remove noise from sculpting or imports.

  20. SCULPTING
        new_geo = MT_mesh.sculptBrush(geom, {
            "centre": [0, 0, 0],
            "radius": 0.5,
            "mode": "smooth",  # "smooth" | "pinch" | "inflate" | "grab"
            "strength": 0.3,
        })
        # Local sculpting brush. Apply iteratively to build
        # up detail. Style A factories rarely use this; Style B
        # sculpt-driven workflows use it heavily.

  21. DISPLACE + NOISE
        new_geo = MT_mesh.displaceSurface(geom, {
            "amplitude": 0.05,
            "frequency": 4.0,
            "octaves": 4,
            "seed": 1,
        })
        # Displace surface vertices along normals using a noise
        # field. Used for organic detail (skin wrinkles, bark
        # texture, etc.).

  Use pattern (Style B):
        from js import MT_mesh, MT_core

        def create_model():
            g = THREE.Group.new()
            # Start with a high-poly sphere, decimate to chunky
            sphere = MT_core.makePrimitive("sphere", {"r": 1, "ws": 24, "hs": 16})
            chunky = MT_mesh.qemDecimate(sphere, {"ratio": 0.05})
            # Add procedural displacement for organic detail
            organic = MT_mesh.displaceSurface(chunky, {
                "amplitude": 0.05, "frequency": 3, "octaves": 3, "seed": 42,
            })
            mesh = THREE.Mesh.new(organic, mat)
            g.add(mesh)
            return g

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 283 — MT.organic (13 ORGANIC HELPERS, PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 121-133)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

The ORGANIC module covers skinning, blendshapes, muscles, hair,
and auto-rigging — the parts of the 3D pipeline that handle
characters, creatures, and articulated models.

  22. SMPL
        skeleton = MT_organic.smplSkeleton({"gender": "neutral"})
        # Standard SMPL skeleton (24 joints, 6 blendshapes).
        mesh = MT_organic.smplBuild(skeleton, {"shape": 0, "pose": 0})
        # Apply shape + pose to get a posed SMPL mesh.
        # Requires the SMPL model file (NOT shipped; user must
        # supply their own).

  23. BLENDSHAPES
        MT_organic.makeBlendshape(target_geom, "smile", 0.5)
        # Add a named blendshape (delta) to a target mesh.
        # The renderer stores these on the mesh's morphAttributes.

  24. SHAPE KEYS
        store = MT_organic.shapeKeyStore(mesh)
        # Capture the current state as a named shape key.
        evaluated = MT_organic.shapeKeyEvaluate(mesh, "smile", 0.5)
        # Evaluate the mesh at a given weight (0..1).

  25. MORPH TARGETS
        computed = MT_organic.morphTargetCompute(geom, "smile", 0.5)
        # Compute the morph-target delta and apply it. Used
        # after face-cap construction (PART 31 sculpt enrichment).

  26. LINEAR BLEND SKINNING (LBS)
        from_js_skeleton = MT_organic.computeLBS(skeleton, geom)
        # Or lbsSkin(geom, joints, weights) for manual control.
        # Most chunky-mascot Python factories skip LBS — they
        # use a hierarchical Group of static meshes (no skinning).

  27. DUAL QUATERNION SKINNING (DQS)
        skinned = MT_organic.computeDQS(skeleton, geom)
        # Or dqsSkin(geom, joints, weights). Better than LBS
        # for character rigs with twisting joints.

  28. IMPLICIT SKINNING
        skinned = MT_organic.implicitSkin(geom, {"envelope": "smooth"})
        # Smooth-envelope skinning. Avoids the LBS "candy
        # wrapper" collapse at joints. Style B character
        # factories use this for natural-looking skin.

  29. DELTA MUSH
        smoothed = MT_organic.deltaMush(geom, {
            "iterations": 3,
            "steps": 5,
        })
        # Post-skinning smoothing. Preserves volume while
        # removing the LBS bulge artifact. Apply after computeLBS.

  30. CAGE DEFORMATION
        cage = MT_organic.buildCage(geom, {"segments": 4})
        deformed = MT_organic.cageDeform(cage, geom, {
            "displacements": [[0, 0.1, 0], ...]  # per-vertex
        })
        # Cage-based deformation. Use for large-scale shape
        # changes without re-skinning.

  31. MUSCLE SIMULATION
        muscles = [
            {"name": "bicep_L", "origin": [0.2, 1.2, 0],
             "insertion": [0.4, 0.6, 0], "radius": 0.05},
            ...
        ]
        MT_organic.muscleSim(muscles, {"bicep_L": 0.7}, {"geom": skinned_mesh})
        # Activate muscles by name → weight. The renderer
        # applies the bulge to the skinned mesh.

  32. SOFT BODY SIMULATION
        soft = MT_organic.softBodySim(geom, {
            "gravity": [0, -9.8, 0],
            "dt": 1/60,
            "iterations": 10,
        })
        # Simulate a soft body (jelly, cloth, deformable
        # surface). Used for organic creatures and slimy
        # surfaces.

  33. HAIR SYSTEM
        hair = MT_organic.hairStrands(skull_mesh, {
            "count": 1000,
            "segments": 8,
            "length": 0.05,
        })
        # Generate hair strands on a mesh surface. Used for
        # creatures with fur / hair (the furry mascot archetype).

  34. AUTO-RIGGING
        rig = MT_organic.autoRig(geom, {"resolution": 32})
        # Auto-generate a skeleton + skinning weights for an
        # arbitrary mesh. Output is a Group with a SkinnedMesh
        # and a hierarchy of bone Groups.

  Use pattern (Style B character factory):
        from js import MT_organic, THREE

        def create_model():
            g = THREE.Group.new()
            g.name = "Rigged Mascot"

            # Build the mesh
            mesh = THREE.Mesh.new(geom, mat)
            g.add(mesh)

            # Auto-rig
            rig = MT_organic.autoRig(geom, {"resolution": 16})
            for bone in rig["bones"]:
                g.add(bone)

            # Animate via tick
            def tick(dt, elapsed):
                for joint_name, angle_fn in [
                    ("shoulder_L", lambda t: math.sin(t * 2) * 0.4),
                    ("elbow_L", lambda t: math.sin(t * 2 + 1) * 0.3),
                ]:
                    if joint_name in rig["bones_dict"]:
                        rig["bones_dict"][joint_name].rotation.x = angle_fn(elapsed)
            g.userData.tick = tick
            return g

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 284 — MT.materials (6 MATERIAL/UV HELPERS, PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 134-139)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  35. PBR MATERIAL NODES
        from js import MT_materials
        mat = MT_materials.pbrMaterialGraph({
            "color": [0.9, 0.5, 0.2],
            "roughness": {"type": "noise", "freq": 4},
            "metalness": {"type": "constant", "value": 0.1},
            "normal": {"type": "bump", "amplitude": 0.05},
        }, {"size": 512})
        # Build a MeshStandardMaterial / MeshPhysicalMaterial
        # from a node graph. The graph can reference any
        # procedural pattern (noise, voronoi, brick, etc.).

  36. PROCEDURAL TEXTURING
        tex = MT_materials.proceduralTextureCanvas({
            "type": "noise",      # "noise" | "voronoi" | "brick" |
                                  # "stripe" | "truchet" | "cairo" |
                                  # "splatter" | "wood" | "marble"
            "seed": 1,
            "scale": 4,
        }, {"size": 512})
        # Returns a CanvasTexture. Apply to material.map.

  37. UV UNWRAPPING
        new_geom = MT_materials.uvUnwrap(geom, {
            "method": "lscm",   # "planar" | "box" | "lscm"
        })
        # Or use PART 234 progressive_uv for visibility-priority
        # UV unwrap. For high-poly meshes, use lscm (least
        # squares conformal mapping).

  38. BAKING
        baked = MT_materials.bakeMap(geom, "normal", {"size": 1024})
        # Or "ao" | "curvature" | "thickness" | "metallic".
        # Returns a Texture. Apply to material.normalMap /
        # material.aoMap / etc.

  39. TEXTURE PAINTING
        canvas = MT_materials.paintTexture(base_canvas, {
            "brush": "round",   # "round" | "flat" | "spray"
            "size": 20,
            "color": [0.5, 0.2, 0.1, 1.0],
            "opacity": 0.8,
        })
        # Paint on a canvas procedurally. Returns the modified
        # canvas. Wrap in CanvasTexture for material.map.

  40. ATLAS PACKING
        packed = MT_materials.packAtlases([
            {"name": "diffuse", "image": diffuse_canvas},
            {"name": "normal",  "image": normal_canvas},
            {"name": "roughness", "image": rough_canvas},
        ], {"width": 2048, "height": 2048, "padding": 4})
        # MaxRects BSSF packing. Returns a single atlas
        # canvas + UV remap data.

  Use pattern (Style B):
        from js import MT_materials, THREE

        def make_worn_metal():
            tex = MT_materials.proceduralTextureCanvas({
                "type": "noise", "seed": 7, "scale": 8,
            }, {"size": 256})
            return THREE.MeshStandardMaterial.new({
                "color": 0x8a6a4a,
                "roughness": 0.5,
                "metalness": 0.85,
                "roughnessMap": tex,
            })

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 285 — MT.advanced (4 ADVANCED HELPERS, PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 140-143)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  41. LOD GENERATION
        from js import MT_advanced
        lods = MT_advanced.generateLOD(geom, {
            "levels": [1.0, 0.5, 0.25, 0.1, 0.05],
            "minVerts": 100,
        })
        # Returns a list of 5 BufferGeometries. Apply via
        # THREE.LOD in Style B.

  42. INSTANCING
        rivets = MT_advanced.makeInstanced(rivet_geo, 8, {
            "transforms": [
                {"position": [math.cos(a) * 0.4, 0, math.sin(a) * 0.4],
                 "rotation": [0, 0, 0], "scale": [1, 1, 1]}
                for a in [i * 2 * math.pi / 8 for i in range(8)]
            ],
        })
        # Returns a single THREE.InstancedMesh. 1 draw call
        # instead of 8. Used for repeated details (rivets,
        # bolts, scales).

  43. GEOMETRY NODES
        result = MT_advanced.geoNodesEvaluate([
            {"type": "primitive", "kind": "cube", "size": 1.0},
            {"type": "transform", "translation": [0, 1, 0]},
            {"type": "instance", "count": 10, "axis": "y"},
            {"type": "merge"},
        ])
        # A "geometry nodes" style stack. Each node takes the
        # output of the previous. Returns a single merged
        # BufferGeometry.

  44. PHYSICS SIMULATION
        from js import MT_advanced
        cloth = MT_advanced.buildClothWorld({
            "width": 1.0, "height": 1.0, "segments": 16,
            "gravity": [0, -9.8, 0],
        })
        # Or buildFluidWorld(...) for fluid sim.
        # The renderer steps the simulation every frame:
        #     g.userData.tick = lambda dt, elapsed: MT_advanced.physicsStep(cloth, dt)
        # Used for cloth-draped characters, fluid simulations,
        # and soft-body dynamics.

  44.5 — MT ENGINE (DISPATCHER) PYTHON FORM
    The MT engine itself (`window.MT`) is also exposed to Python:

        from js import MT
        # Dispatch by name
        geo = MT.run("makePrimitive", "box", {"w": 1, "h": 1, "d": 1})
        # Or by PART number
        geo = MT.run(100, None, {"kind": "box"})   # PART 100 = primitive
        # Run a stack
        final_geo = MT.runStack({
            "target": geom,
            "steps": [
                {"technique": "displaceSurface", "opts": {"amplitude": 0.05}},
                {"technique": "qemDecimate",     "opts": {"ratio": 0.3}},
            ],
        })
        # Apply to every mesh in a scene
        MT.applyToScene("laplacianSmooth", scene, {"iterations": 2})
        # Self-test (16-bundle sanity check)
        results = MT.selfTest()

  The `MT.catalog` is the canonical list of all 44 techniques.
  Use `MT.catalog[i]` to look up the i-th technique's name and
  signature.

================================================================================
PART 286-287 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: PBR RECIPES + CROSS-PRIMITIVE
================================================================================

The standalone techniques from PART 45 (CS2 PBR profile) + PART 67
(cross-primitive vocabulary) + PART 74 (procedural texturing
recipes) + PART 68-71 (Star/Polygon/Annulus/etc. primitives) are
shipped here in Python form. These are the "material + shape"
techniques that the AI factory uses to express non-default
materials and shapes.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 286 — PART 74 PBR CREATURE RECIPES (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 74)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  4 PBR creature recipes from PART 74, with Python forms:

  1. CHITIN (insect shell)
        from js import THREE, MT_materials
        def make_chitin(base_color=[0.15, 0.10, 0.05]):
            tex = MT_materials.proceduralTextureCanvas({
                "type": "voronoi", "scale": 16, "seed": 1,
            }, {"size": 512})
            return THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial.new({
                "color": base_color,
                "roughness": 0.35,
                "metalness": 0.2,
                "iridescence": 1.0,
                "iridescenceIOR": 1.3,
                "iridescenceThicknessRange": [100, 800],
                "clearcoat": 0.6,
                "clearcoatRoughness": 0.1,
                "roughnessMap": tex,
            })

  2. ELYTRA (beetle shell — same as chitin but with sharper iridescence)
        def make_elytra(base_color=[0.05, 0.15, 0.30]):
            tex = MT_materials.proceduralTextureCanvas({
                "type": "voronoi", "scale": 32, "seed": 7,
            }, {"size": 1024})
            return THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial.new({
                "color": base_color,
                "roughness": 0.2,
                "metalness": 0.3,
                "iridescence": 1.0,
                "iridescenceIOR": 1.5,
                "iridescenceThicknessRange": [200, 1200],
                "clearcoat": 0.8,
                "clearcoatRoughness": 0.05,
            })

  3. MEMBRANE (insect wing — translucent)
        from js import document
        def make_membrane(base_color=[0.85, 0.85, 0.95]):
            tex_canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
            tex_canvas.width = 256
            tex_canvas.height = 256
            ctx = tex_canvas.getContext("2d")
            # Draw vein pattern
            ctx.strokeStyle = "rgba(0,0,0,0.3)"
            ctx.lineWidth = 0.5
            for i in range(8):
                ctx.beginPath()
                ctx.moveTo(128, 128)
                ctx.lineTo(128 + math.cos(i * math.pi / 4) * 100,
                           128 + math.sin(i * math.pi / 4) * 100)
                ctx.stroke()
            tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(tex_canvas)
            return THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial.new({
                "color": base_color,
                "roughness": 0.7,
                "metalness": 0.0,
                "transmission": 0.85,
                "thickness": 0.05,
                "ior": 1.4,
                "transparent": True,
                "opacity": 0.6,
                "side": THREE.DoubleSide,
                "map": tex,
            })

  4. VELVET (sheen surface)
        from js import THREE
        def make_velvet(base_color=[0.4, 0.05, 0.15]):
            return THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial.new({
                "color": base_color,
                "roughness": 0.95,
                "metalness": 0.0,
                "sheen": 1.0,
                "sheenColor": 0xffffff,
                "sheenRoughness": 0.3,
            })

  Other PART 74 procedural texturing recipes (Python forms):
      - microRoughnessMap: PART 245.1
      - punctate (cavity dirt): use MT_materials.bakeMap(geom, "ao", size=256)
      - striate (brushed metal): use MT_materials.proceduralTextureCanvas(
            {"type": "stripe", "scale": 64, "angle": 0.1}, size=512)
      - F2-F1 Worley: MT_materials.proceduralTextureCanvas(
            {"type": "voronoi", "scale": 16, "mode": "F2_F1"}, size=512)
      - domain warp: combine 2 noise patterns via MT_materials.bakeMap
        then mix in post-processing

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 287 — PART 67 CROSS-PRIMITIVE VOCABULARY (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 67)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 7 cross-primitive shape generators from PART 67:
