  1. DISC (flat circular mesh)
        from js import MT_core
        geo = MT_core.makePrimitive("disc", {"rInner": 0.5, "rOuter": 1.0,
                                              "segments": 32})
        # Returns a flat annulus geometry. Useful for wheels,
        # halos, decorative rings.

  2. RING (3D ring)
        geo = MT_core.makePrimitive("ring", {"r": 0.5, "tube": 0.05,
                                             "segments": 32})
        # Returns a TorusGeometry. Same as THREE.TorusGeometry.

  3. ARC (partial ring)
        geo = MT_core.makePrimitive("arc", {"r": 0.5, "tube": 0.05,
                                             "arc": math.pi,
                                             "segments": 32})
        # Returns a half-torus. Useful for handlebars, hoops.

  4. HEMISPHERE (half-sphere)
        geo = MT_core.makePrimitive("hemisphere", {"r": 1, "segments": 16})
        # Returns a SphereGeometry with thetaLength = π.

  5. RIBBON (flat strip with width)
        geo = MT_core.makePrimitive("ribbon", {
            "points": [[0, 0, 0], [1, 0.5, 0], [2, 0, 0]],
            "width": 0.1, "segments": 32,
        })
        # Returns a flat ribbon mesh along a polyline. Used for
        # straps, banners, cables.

  6. CROSS-PLANES (4 perpendicular planes)
        geo = MT_core.makePrimitive("crossPlanes", {"size": 1})
        # 4 planes at 0°, 45°, 90°, 135° rotation. Used for
        # billboarding / impostors.

  7. HELIX (3D spiral)
        geo = MT_core.makePrimitive("helix", {
            "r": 0.5, "height": 2, "turns": 3, "tube": 0.02,
            "segments": 64,
        })
        # Returns a TubeGeometry along a helix curve. Used
        # for springs, spirals, helical cables.

  6 deform modifiers (PART 67):
      - taper:   MT_meshes.taper(geom, "y", 0.5)
      - twist:   MT_meshes.twist(geom, "y", math.pi)
      - bend:    MT_meshes.bend(geom, "x", math.pi / 4)
      - spherize: MT_meshes.spherize(geom, 0.5)
      - inflate: MT_meshes.inflate(geom, 0.1)
      - noise:   MT_meshes.applyNoise(geom, {"amplitude": 0.05,
                                              "frequency": 4})

      rng = MT_materials.mulberry32(42)

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 289 — PART 45 CS2 PBR PROFILE (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 45)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The CS2 PBR profile (9 finish styles + 5-tier wear system +
  4 new MeshPhysicalMaterial properties) in Python form. Most
  Python factories skip CS2 unless the model is a weapon, blade,
  or metal-finish subject.

  9 finish styles:
      - "Anodized"        — metalness=0.95, roughness=0.4, clearcoat=0.5
      - "Anodized Multicolored Custom Paint Job" — iridescence=1.0
      - "Gunsmith"        — high metalness, varied roughness
      - "Hydrographic"    — pattern-based, see PART 45
      - "Patina"          — heavy wear + oxidation
      - "Solid Color"     — basic paint
      - "Sprayed"         — sprayed paint texture
      - "Damascus Steel"  — wavy pattern, high metalness
      - "Case Hardened"   — color-shifting via iridescence

  Python form:
      from js import THREE
      from js import MT_materials
      mat = MT_materials.cs2PbrProfile({
          "finish": "Anodized",
          "color": [0.6, 0.2, 0.1],
          "wear": 0.3,  # 0 = factory new, 1 = battle-scarred
          "seed": 42,
      })
      # Returns a MeshPhysicalMaterial with the 9 finish +
      # 5-tier wear system applied.

  Apply wear:
      MT_materials.applyWear(mat, 0.5)  # bump wear to 50%
      MT_materials.cs2WearMaskTexture(mat, 0.5)

  4 new MeshPhysicalMaterial properties (CS2):
      - iridescence:        0..1, enables iridescence layer
      - iridescenceIOR:     1.0..2.333, index of refraction
      - iridescenceThicknessRange: [min, max] in nm
      - specularIntensity:  0..1, F0 multiplier

  5-tier wear:
      - "FN" (factory new, wear=0)
      - "MW" (minimal wear, wear=0.15)
      - "FT" (field-tested, wear=0.4)
      - "WW" (well-worn, wear=0.7)
      - "BS" (battle-scarred, wear=1.0)

  Apply via the meta block:
      bp = lbl.blueprint()
      bp["meta"]["cs2Finish"] = "Anodized"
      bp["meta"]["cs2Wear"] = 0.3
      return bp

  Python validators fire ONLY when meta.cs2Finish is set
  (PART 45 audit E30). Non-CS2 models are unchanged.

PART 290-291 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: ADVANCED SHADERS + CONSTRAINTS
================================================================================

The PART 28 advanced shader & VFX techniques + PART 27 declarative
constraints in Python form. These are for power-user Python
factories that want full shader control or declarative constraints.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 290 — PART 28 ADVANCED SHADER & VFX TECHNIQUES (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 28)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  1. CUSTOM SHADER MATERIAL
        from js import THREE
        mat = THREE.ShaderMaterial.new({
            "vertexShader": """
                varying vec3 vPos;
                void main() {
                    vPos = position;
                    gl_Position = projectionMatrix * modelViewMatrix *
                                  vec4(position, 1.0);
                }
            """,
            "fragmentShader": """
                varying vec3 vPos;
                void main() {
                    gl_FragColor = vec4(abs(sin(vPos.x * 10.0)), 0.5, 0.5, 1.0);
                }
            """,
            "uniforms": {"time": {"value": 0.0}},
        })
        mesh = THREE.Mesh.new(geom, mat)
        # Update the time uniform in tick:
        def tick(dt, elapsed):
            mat.uniforms["time"]["value"] = elapsed

  2. POST-PROCESSING (OutlinePass, Bloom, etc.)
        from js import MT_lookdev
        outline = MT_lookdev.makeOutlinePass({
            "edgeStrength": 3.0,
            "edgeGlow": 0.5,
            "edgeThickness": 1.0,
            "pulsePeriod": 0,
            "visibleEdgeColor": 0xffffff,
            "hiddenEdgeColor": 0x22090a,
        })
        # The renderer auto-applies OutlinePass for chunky-mascot
        # models; this is the manual override path.

  3. PARTICLE SYSTEMS
        particles = MT_lookdev.makeParticleSystem({
            "count": 1000,
            "lifetime": 2.0,
            "velocity": [0, 1, 0],
            "spread": 0.5,
            "color": 0xff8800,
            "size": 0.02,
        })
        g.add(particles)

  4. SHADOWS (PCFSoftShadowMap, bias, etc.)
        from js import lblSpec
        lblSpec.renderer.shadowMap.enabled = True
        lblSpec.renderer.shadowMap.type = THREE.PCFSoftShadowMap
        # Cast/receive shadow on every body-part mesh:
        for mesh in g.children:
            mesh.castShadow = True
            mesh.receiveShadow = True

  5. POST-FX
        from js import MT_lookdev
        bloom = MT_lookdev.makeBloomPass({"strength": 0.5, "radius": 0.4,
                                          "threshold": 0.85})
        ssao = MT_lookdev.makeSSAOPass({"kernelRadius": 16, "minDistance": 0.005,
                                         "maxDistance": 0.1})
        # Add to the renderer's post-FX chain
        lblSpec.composer.addPass(bloom)
        lblSpec.composer.addPass(ssao)

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 291 — PART 27 DECLARATIVE CONSTRAINTS (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 27)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  Declarative + procedural constraints for Python factories. Use
  these when the model has hard requirements (a "must touch" or
  "must not overlap" rule) that the AI needs to enforce.

  1. ATTACH (one part must touch another)
        from js import lblSpec
        lblSpec.constraints.attach({
            "child": "head",
            "parent": "body",
            "snapPoint": "body.top",
            "childPoint": "head.bottom",
        })

  2. NO_OVERLAP (two parts must not intersect)
        lblSpec.constraints.noOverlap({
            "parts": ["wheel_L", "wheel_R"],
            "margin": 0.01,
        })

  3. KEEP_RATIO (two parts must keep a fixed aspect ratio)
        lblSpec.constraints.keepRatio({
            "parts": ["body", "limb"],
            "ratio": 2.0,    # body.length = 2 * limb.length
            "tolerance": 0.05,
        })

  4. SNAP_TO_GRID
        lblSpec.constraints.snapToGrid({
            "parts": ["all"],
            "gridSize": 0.05,
        })

  5. ALIGN_AXIS
        lblSpec.constraints.alignAxis({
            "parts": ["wheel_L", "wheel_R"],
            "axis": "y",
        })

  Procedural constraints (computed at runtime):
      - distance(part_a, part_b, fn) — keep two parts at a
        function-driven distance
      - orbit(part, center, radius, speed) — orbit one part
        around another

  Example — a chunky 2-wheeled cart with declarative constraints:
      from js import lblSpec
      lblSpec.constraints.attach({"child": "body", "parent": "axle"})
      lblSpec.constraints.noOverlap({"parts": ["wheel_L", "wheel_R"]})
      lblSpec.constraints.keepRatio({
          "parts": ["body", "axle"],
          "ratio": 1.5,  # body.length = 1.5 * axle.length
          "tolerance": 0.05,
      })

PART 292-293 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: RIGGING + SCULPT ENRICHMENT

The PART 30 rigging & animation authoring standard + PART 31
character/mascot sculpt enrichment in Python form.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 292 — PART 30 RIGGING & ANIMATION AUTHORING (PYTHON FORM)
(amends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 30)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 5-bone chain pattern for a chunky character, in Python:

      from js import THREE, MT_organic

      def build_chunky_rig():
          # 5 bones: pelvis → spine → chest → neck → head
          pelvis = THREE.Bone.new(); pelvis.name = "pelvis"
          spine = THREE.Bone.new(); spine.name = "spine"
          chest = THREE.Bone.new(); chest.name = "chest"
          neck = THREE.Bone.new(); neck.name = "neck"
          head = THREE.Bone.new(); head.name = "head"
          # Add to a skeleton
          skeleton = THREE.Skeleton.new([pelvis, spine, chest, neck, head])
          # Position the bones
          pelvis.position.set(0, 0, 0)
          spine.position.set(0, 0.3, 0)
          chest.position.set(0, 0.6, 0)
          neck.position.set(0, 0.9, 0)
          head.position.set(0, 1.1, 0)
          return skeleton

  Skeletal skinning (Style B):
      from js import THREE
      skinned_mesh = THREE.SkinnedMesh.new(geom, mat)
      skeleton = build_chunky_rig()
      for bone in skeleton.bones:
          skinned_mesh.add(bone)
      skinned_mesh.bind(skeleton, skeleton.bones)
      # Now you can rotate any bone to deform the mesh.

  85-bone skeleton (the canonical character rig):
      See PART 30.1 in Prompt_To_Ts.txt for the full 85-bone
      hierarchy. The Python equivalent follows the same pattern.

  5-LOD chain:
      from js import MT_advanced
      lods = MT_advanced.generateLOD(geom, {
          "levels": [1.0, 0.5, 0.25, 0.1, 0.05],
          "minVerts": 100,
      })
      lod_obj = THREE.LOD.new()
      for i, level_geo in enumerate(lods):
          lod_obj.addLevel(THREE.Mesh.new(level_geo, mat), i * 10)
      g.add(lod_obj)
      # The renderer auto-switches LOD based on camera distance.

PART 293 — PART 31 CHARACTER / MASCOT SCULPT ENRICHMENT (PYTHON FORM)

  The 5-layer character production pipeline in Python form. This
  is the chunky-mascot sculpt enrichment that makes a model feel

      from js import MT_materials

      from js import MT_advanced

                                          {"transforms": [...]})

      from js import MT_lookdev

      # Layers 1-3 are handled by the lbl helper chain.
      # Layer 4 (accessories) uses lbl.attach_box / cylinder.
      # Layer 5 (lighting) is renderer-side auto-applied.
      # See Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 31 for the Style A form.

  60/25/15 polygon distribution (PART 31.6):

      - SECONDARY (25%, 750): torso, arms, legs, boots, eye patch
      - TERTIARY (15%, 450): rivets, cheek bolts, antenna, harness
      Python form (Style A): see lbl.define_palette + the
      detailInventory on the returned blueprint.

  Production-completeness check (PART 31.7):
      from js import lblSpec
      report = lblSpec.auditProductionCompleteness(g)
      if report["missing_identity_parts"]:
          # Add the missing parts before returning
          ...

  7 production techniques (PART 31.8):

PART 296-302 — v1.36 / v8.27 PYTHON ADAPTATION: COMPLETE PROCEDURAL CANVAS TEXTURES
================================================================================

PART 8 of Prompt_To_Ts.txt covers procedural canvas textures in
detail — the canonical recipe for any model that needs a procedural
texture instead of an external image asset. The Python adaptation
already has PART 209 (the basic recipe). PART 296-302 here covers
the rest: the 7 procedural patterns, 3 micro-roughness patterns,
4 height-to-X conversions, the mulberry32 determinism contract,
and the F2-F1 Worley technique.

This is the missing layer the original v1.36 / v8.27 release
needed — Python factories had PART 209 but not the full PART 8
menu. With PART 296-302, Python can produce any procedural texture
the renderer supports.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 296 — 7 PROCEDURAL PATTERNS (PYTHON FORM)
(extends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 8.2)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  1. NOISE (perlin / value / fbm / worley)
       from js import document, THREE
       def make_noise_texture(size=256, kind="perlin", scale=4, seed=1):
           canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           canvas.width = size; canvas.height = size
           ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
           state = [seed & 0xFFFFFFFF]
           def rand():
               state[0] = (state[0] + 0x6D2B79F5) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               t = state[0]
               t = ((t ^ (t >> 15)) * (t | 1)) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               t = (t ^ (t + (((t ^ (t >> 7)) * (t | 61)) & 0xFFFFFFFF))) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               return ((t ^ (t >> 14)) & 0xFFFFFFFF) / 4294967296
           cell = size // scale
           for y in range(scale):
               for x in range(scale):
                   v = int(rand() * 256)
                   ctx.fillStyle = f"rgb({v},{v},{v})"
                   ctx.fillRect(x*cell, y*cell, cell, cell)
           tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)
           tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.RepeatWrapping
           return tex

  2. VORONOI (Worley noise)
       def make_voronoit_texture(size=256, scale=8, seed=1):
           # Generate seed points, then for each pixel find
           # the nearest seed (F1) and 2nd nearest (F2). The
           # difference F2-F1 gives the cell-edge intensity.
           canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           canvas.width = size; canvas.height = size
           ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
           img_data = ctx.createImageData(size, size)
           # Seed points
           seeds = [(rand()*size, rand()*size) for _ in range(scale*scale)]
           for y in range(size):
               for x in range(size):
                   distances = sorted(
                       ((x - sx)**2 + (y - sy)**2 for sx, sy in seeds)
                   )
                   f1, f2 = distances[0], distances[1]
                   edge = max(0, min(255, int(255 * (1 - (f2 - f1) / scale))))
                   idx = (y * size + x) * 4
                   img_data.data[idx] = edge
                   img_data.data[idx+1] = edge
                   img_data.data[idx+2] = edge
                   img_data.data[idx+3] = 255
           ctx.putImageData(img_data, 0, 0)
           tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)
           tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.RepeatWrapping
           return tex

  3. BRICK (running-bond pattern)
       def make_brick_texture(size=256, rows=8, cols=16,
                              brick_color="#8a4a3a",
                              mortar_color="#3a2a1a"):
           canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           canvas.width = size; canvas.height = size
           ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
           ctx.fillStyle = mortar_color
           ctx.fillRect(0, 0, size, size)
           row_h = size / rows
           col_w = size / cols
           for row in range(rows):
               offset = (col_w / 2) if row % 2 == 1 else 0
               for col in range(-1, cols + 1):
                   x = col * col_w + offset
                   y = row * row_h
                   ctx.fillStyle = brick_color
                   ctx.fillRect(x + 1, y + 1, col_w - 2, row_h - 2)
           tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)
           tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.RepeatWrapping
           return tex

  4. STRIPE (parallel lines)
       def make_stripe_texture(size=256, count=8,
                               c1="#222", c2="#aaa",
                               angle=0):
           canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           canvas.width = size; canvas.height = size
           ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
           ctx.translate(size/2, size/2)
           ctx.rotate(angle)
           ctx.translate(-size/2, -size/2)
           stripe_h = size / count
           for i in range(count):
               ctx.fillStyle = c1 if i % 2 == 0 else c2
               ctx.fillRect(0, i*stripe_h, size, stripe_h)
           tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)
           tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.RepeatWrapping
           return tex

  5. TRUCHET (random tile curves)
       def make_truchet_texture(size=256, count=8,
                                c1="#1a1a1a", c2="#f0e8d0"):
           canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           canvas.width = size; canvas.height = size
           ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
           ctx.fillStyle = c1
           ctx.fillRect(0, 0, size, size)
           cell = size / count
           for row in range(count):
               for col in range(count):
                   x = col * cell; y = row * cell
                   ctx.fillStyle = c2
                   if rand() < 0.5:
                       ctx.beginPath()
                       ctx.arc(x, y, cell/2, 0, math.pi/2)
                       ctx.arc(x+cell, y+cell, cell/2, math.pi, 3*math.pi/2)
                       ctx.fill()
                   else:
                       ctx.beginPath()
                       ctx.arc(x+cell, y, cell/2, math.pi/2, math.pi)
                       ctx.arc(x, y+cell, cell/2, 3*math.pi/2, 2*math.pi)
                       ctx.fill()
           tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)
           tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.RepeatWrapping
           return tex

  6. CAIRO (pentagonal tiling)
       from js import document
       def make_cairo_texture(size=256, count=6, color="#e8d0a0"):
           # Cairo tiling uses irregular pentagons. The
           # implementation is non-trivial; for the canonical
           # recipe see PART 8.2.6 in Prompt_To_Ts.txt.
           pass  # See the lbl helper for a working impl.

  7. SPLATTER (random blobs)
       def make_splatter_texture(size=256, count=20,
                                 c1="#222", c2="#f5821b",
                                 blob_min=4, blob_max=20):
           canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           canvas.width = size; canvas.height = size
           ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
           ctx.fillStyle = c1
           ctx.fillRect(0, 0, size, size)
           ctx.fillStyle = c2
           for _ in range(count):
               cx, cy = rand()*size, rand()*size
               r = blob_min + rand() * (blob_max - blob_min)
               ctx.beginPath()
               ctx.arc(cx, cy, r, 0, 2*math.pi)
               ctx.fill()
           tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)
           tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.RepeatWrapping
           return tex

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 297 — 3 MICRO-ROUGHNESS PATTERNS (PYTHON FORM)
(extends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 8.3)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 3 micro-roughness patterns (punctate, striate, micro-roughness
  map) from PART 8.3, with Python forms.

  1. PUNCTATE (small dots for material variation)
       def make_punctate_roughness(size=256, density=64):
           canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           canvas.width = size; canvas.height = size
           ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
           ctx.fillStyle = "#808080"  # baseline roughness 0.5
           ctx.fillRect(0, 0, size, size)
           ctx.fillStyle = "#ffffff"  # bright spots (rougher)
           for _ in range(density):
               cx, cy = rand()*size, rand()*size
               r = 1 + rand() * 3
               ctx.beginPath()
               ctx.arc(cx, cy, r, 0, 2*math.pi)
               ctx.fill()
           return THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)

  2. STRIATE (parallel grooves for brushed metal)
       def make_striate_roughness(size=256, count=200,
                                  angle=0.01):
           canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           canvas.width = size; canvas.height = size
           ctx = canvas.getContext("2d")
           ctx.fillStyle = "#888"  # mid roughness
           ctx.fillRect(0, 0, size, size)
           ctx.translate(size/2, size/2)
           ctx.rotate(angle)
           ctx.translate(-size/2, -size/2)
           for _ in range(count):
               y = rand() * size
               w = size * (0.5 + rand() * 0.5)
               x = (rand() - 0.5) * size
               gray = 0x40 + int(rand() * 0xc0)
               ctx.fillStyle = f"rgb({gray},{gray},{gray})"
               ctx.fillRect(x, y, w, 1)
           return THREE.CanvasTexture.new(canvas)

  3. MICRO-ROUGHNESS (256×256 noise → 4×4 RepeatWrapping)
       # The canonical PART 8.1 recipe — see Prompt_To_Py.txt
       # PART 258.1 for the full Python form.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 298 — 4 HEIGHT-TO-X CONVERSIONS (PYTHON FORM)
(extends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 8.4)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 4 height-to-X conversions from PART 8.4:

  1. HEIGHT → NORMAL (Sobel operator)
       def height_to_normal(height_canvas, strength=1.0):
           size = height_canvas.width
           src_ctx = height_canvas.getContext("2d")
           src_data = src_ctx.getImageData(0, 0, size, size).data
           dst_canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           dst_canvas.width = size; dst_canvas.height = size
           dst_ctx = dst_canvas.getContext("2d")
           dst_data = dst_ctx.createImageData(size, size)
           for y in range(size):
               for x in range(size):
                   tl = src_data[(y * size + max(0, x-1)) * 4] / 255
                   tr = src_data[(y * size + min(size-1, x+1)) * 4] / 255
                   tu = src_data[(max(0, y-1) * size + x) * 4] / 255
                   td = src_data[(min(size-1, y+1) * size + x) * 4] / 255
                   dx = (tr - tl) * strength
                   dy = (td - tu) * strength
                   nz = 1.0
                   length = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy + nz*nz)
                   nx, ny, nzz = dx/length, dy/length, nz/length
                   idx = (y * size + x) * 4
                   dst_data.data[idx]   = int((nx * 0.5 + 0.5) * 255)
                   dst_data.data[idx+1] = int((ny * 0.5 + 0.5) * 255)
                   dst_data.data[idx+2] = int((nzz * 0.5 + 0.5) * 255)
                   dst_data.data[idx+3] = 255
           dst_ctx.putImageData(dst_data, 0, 0)
           return THREE.CanvasTexture.new(dst_canvas)

  2. HEIGHT → AO (cavity mask)
       # Cavities (low height) are darker (occluded).
       ao_data = src_data  # just remap: low → 0, high → 255
       # Then blur for soft AO.
       # The lbl helper provides lbl.cavity_aware_pattern(..., mode="ao")
       # which is the canonical recipe.

  3. HEIGHT → ROUGHNESS (smooth material variation)
       # Map the height to roughness directly. Use the
       # normalized height as the roughness value.
       # Smooth areas → high roughness, sharp areas → low roughness.
       # See MT_materials.bakeMap(geom, "roughness") for the
       # automated version.

  4. HEIGHT → CURVATURE (F2-F1 Worley)
       # Use the Voronoi F2-F1 distance as the curvature
       # measure. See PART 296.2 for the Voronoi recipe.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 299 — MULBERRY32 DETERMINISM CONTRACT (PYTHON FORM)
(extends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 8.1)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The mulberry32 PRNG is the canonical determinism contract for
  procedural Python factories. The recipe:

       def mulberry32(seed):
           state = [seed & 0xFFFFFFFF]
           def f():
               state[0] = (state[0] + 0x6D2B79F5) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               t = state[0]
               t = ((t ^ (t >> 15)) * (t | 1)) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               t = (t ^ (t + (((t ^ (t >> 7)) * (t | 61)) & 0xFFFFFFFF))) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               return ((t ^ (t >> 14)) & 0xFFFFFFFF) / 4294967296
           return f

  Same as TS — both languages produce identical sequences for the
  same seed. This means a Python factory with mulberry32(42) and a
  TS factory with mulberry32(42) produce the same procedural
  pattern. Critical for cross-format determinism (PART 8.5).

  Test:
       rng = mulberry32(42)
       assert rng() == 0.6766635134574026
       assert rng() == 0.4989308863532245

  Sub-seed derivation (for variant generation):
       def derive_sub_seed(parent_seed, name):
           # FNV-1a hash
           h = 0x811c9dc5
           for c in str(parent_seed) + ":" + name:
               h ^= ord(c)
               h = (h * 0x01000193) & 0xFFFFFFFF
           return h

  Use:
       variant_a_seed = derive_sub_seed(42, "variant_a")
       rng_a = mulberry32(variant_a_seed)
       # variant_a is deterministic + independent of variant_b

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
PART 300 — 4 PBR CREATURE RECIPES (PYTHON FORM)
(extends PART 286 above + Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 74)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 4 PBR creature recipes from PART 286, expanded with full
  Python implementations. The recipes are the canonical materials
  for creature archetypes: insect (chitin), beetle (elytra), wing
  (membrane), fur (velvet).

  1. CHITIN (insect exoskeleton — iridescent)
       from js import THREE, MT_materials
       def make_chitin(base_color=(0.15, 0.10, 0.05),
                       iridescence_strength=1.0):
           tex = MT_materials.proceduralTextureCanvas({
               "type": "voronoi", "scale": 16, "seed": 1,
           }, {"size": 512})
           return THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial.new({
               "color": list(base_color),
               "roughness": 0.35,
               "metalness": 0.2,
               "iridescence": iridescence_strength,
               "iridescenceIOR": 1.3,
               "iridescenceThicknessRange": [100, 800],
               "clearcoat": 0.6,
               "clearcoatRoughness": 0.1,
               "roughnessMap": tex,
           })

  2. ELYTRA (beetle shell — sharper iridescence)
       def make_elytra(base_color=(0.05, 0.15, 0.30),
                       iridescence_thickness=(200, 1200)):
           tex = MT_materials.proceduralTextureCanvas({
               "type": "voronoi", "scale": 32, "seed": 7,
           }, {"size": 1024})
           return THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial.new({
               "color": list(base_color),
               "roughness": 0.2,
               "metalness": 0.3,
               "iridescence": 1.0,
               "iridescenceIOR": 1.5,
               "iridescenceThicknessRange": list(iridescence_thickness),
               "clearcoat": 0.8,
               "clearcoatRoughness": 0.05,
           })

  3. MEMBRANE (insect wing — translucent)
       from js import THREE, document
       import math
       def make_membrane(base_color=(0.85, 0.85, 0.95),
                         vein_count=8):
           tex_canvas = document.createElement("canvas")
           tex_canvas.width = 256; tex_canvas.height = 256
           ctx = tex_canvas.getContext("2d")
           # Veins
           ctx.fillStyle = "rgba(180, 180, 200, 0.6)"
           ctx.fillRect(0, 0, 256, 256)
           ctx.strokeStyle = "rgba(0,0,0,0.5)"
           ctx.lineWidth = 0.5
           for i in range(vein_count):
               angle = i * math.pi / vein_count
               ctx.beginPath()
               ctx.moveTo(128, 128)
               ctx.lineTo(128 + math.cos(angle) * 100,
                         128 + math.sin(angle) * 100)
               ctx.stroke()
           # Cross-veins
           for i in range(vein_count):
               for t in (0.3, 0.6):
                   cx = 128 + math.cos(i * math.pi / vein_count) * 100 * t
                   cy = 128 + math.sin(i * math.pi / vein_count) * 100 * t
                   ctx.beginPath()
                   ctx.moveTo(cx, cy)
                   ctx.lineTo(cx - 15, cy + 8)
                   ctx.stroke()
           tex = THREE.CanvasTexture.new(tex_canvas)
           tex.wrapS = tex.wrapT = THREE.RepeatWrapping
           return THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial.new({
               "color": list(base_color),
               "roughness": 0.7,
               "metalness": 0.0,
               "transmission": 0.85,
               "thickness": 0.05,
               "ior": 1.4,
               "transparent": True,
               "opacity": 0.6,
               "side": THREE.DoubleSide,
               "map": tex,
           })

  4. VELVET (sheen surface)
       def make_velvet(base_color=(0.4, 0.05, 0.15)):
           return THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial.new({
               "color": list(base_color),
               "roughness": 0.95,
               "metalness": 0.0,
               "sheen": 1.0,
               "sheenColor": 0xffffff,
               "sheenRoughness": 0.3,
           })

  When to use each:
    - Chitin: any hard-shelled creature (beetle, crab, turtle)
    - Elytra: beetle wings (the iridescent shell-back wings)
    - Membrane: dragonfly wings, fairy wings, glass-like surfaces
    - Velvet: fur, fabric, soft toys

PART 302 — 4 DETERMINISM CONTRACT ITEMS (PYTHON FORM)
(extends Prompt_To_Ts.txt PART 74.10.4)
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  The 4 determinism contract items:

  1. mulberry32 (canonical PRNG)
       # See PART 299 above.

  2. splitmix32 (alternative PRNG, used for variant generation)
       def splitmix32(seed):
           state = [seed & 0xFFFFFFFF]
           def f():
               state[0] = (state[0] + 0x9E3779B9) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               z = state[0]
               z = ((z ^ (z >> 16)) * 0x85ebca6b) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               z = ((z ^ (z >> 13)) * 0xc2b2ae35) & 0xFFFFFFFF
               return ((z ^ (z >> 16)) & 0xFFFFFFFF) / 4294967296
           return f

  3. deriveSubSeed (FNV-1a hash for variant naming)
       # See PART 299 above.

  4. FNV-1a hash (raw hash function)
       def fnv1a(s):
           h = 0x811c9dc5
           for c in str(s):
               h ^= ord(c)
               h = (h * 0x01000193) & 0xFFFFFFFF
           return h

  The 4 determinism contract items together guarantee that any
  Python factory with the same seed produces the same procedural
  pattern, the same variant naming, and the same hash values
  as a TS factory with the same inputs. This is the cross-format
  determinism contract (PART 8.5).

       from js import THREE
       def make_matte_plastic(color=(0.5, 0.5, 0.5)):
           return THREE.MeshStandardMaterial.new({

               "roughness": 0.7,

       def make_glossy_plastic(color=(0.2, 0.2, 0.2)):

       def make_brushed_metal(color=(0.7, 0.7, 0.7)):

       def make_chrome():

       def make_eye_materials(pupil_color=(0, 0, 0),
                              sclera_color=(1, 1, 1),
                              glow_color=(0.4, 0.8, 1.0)):

       def make_hair_fur(base_color=(0.3, 0.2, 0.1)):

PART 308 — PBR PRESET QUICK-REFERENCE TABLE
──────────────────────────────────────────────

  14 PBR presets at a glance (Python forms above):

    #   PRESET                USE CASE                KEY FIELDS
    1   Matte plastic         Toy body, prop          rough=0.7 metal=0.05
    2   Glossy plastic        Vehicle fairing         rough=0.3 + clearcoat
    3   Brushed metal         Chassis, exhaust        rough=0.4 metal=0.9
    4   Chrome                Trim, mirrors           rough=0.05 metal=1.0
    5   Anodized metal        Premium metal           clearcoat=0.5
    6   Eyes (3 mats)         Character face          emissive glow
    7   Hair / fur            Creature                sheen=1.0
    8   Skin                  Human/creature          transmission=0.05
    9   Glass                 Window, jar             transmission=1.0 ior=1.5
    10  Glow                  Lamp, screen, lava      emissive intensity
    11  Chitin / elytra       Insect shell            iridescence=1.0
    12  Velvet                Fur, fabric             sheen=1.0 sheenRoughness=0.3
    13  Stone / concrete      Architecture            rough=0.95 + aoMap
    14  Wood                  Architecture, prop      rough=0.8 + map

  Compose (mixing 2+ presets on the same model):
      - Set meta.style to the dominant preset.
      - Use the lbl.define_palette() form for Style A; the
        material variables for Style B.
      - Cross-preset transitions (a glossy plastic head on a
        matte plastic body) are valid — just make sure the
        transition is at a natural seam (the neck, the waist,
        etc.) not in the middle of a continuous surface.
